Section <a href="#sec:three-clause-refinements" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:three-clause-refinements">7</a> specializes the cap constants to three clauses and transfers the proper-clause concentration to the Poisson clause law used in the threshold companion. After that main argument, Appendix <a href="#sec:replacement-sharpness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:replacement-sharpness">8</a> determines the limits of the two estimates for arbitrary assignment sets. Appendix <a href="#sec:direct-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:direct-comparison">9</a> compares proper-clause models at different sizes, and Appendix <a href="#sec:appendix-c" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:appendix-c">10</a> gives a second three-clause proof based on prefix exposure and incidence truncation.

<a id="sec:replacement"></a>
### Survival of a set of assignments

The deletion argument will leave a set of assignments that must pass random tests of length $k-1$. We need to relate the probability that those tests eliminate the set to its lifetime under clauses of length $k$. This section proves the relation for every assignment set. Replacing a shorter constraint by a block of clauses of the original length has a qualitative predecessor in Friedgut’s half-cube argument (Friedgut 1999, Lemma 5.7) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Friedgut](#ref-Friedgut)].

<a id="eq:set-survival"></a>
Fix integers $k\ge3$ and $u\ge k$, and put $$r=k-1,\qquad \beta=\frac{k}{k-1}.$$ A proper $s$-clause, for $1\le s\le u$, chooses an $s$-element subset of the $u$ variables uniformly and then chooses independent fair signs on that subset. Clauses are independent, so a whole clause may occur more than once. A collection of clauses *kills* $S\subseteq\{0,1\}^u$ when no member of $S$ satisfies every clause in the collection. For nonnegative integers $d,g$, define $$q_d(S)=\mathbb P(\text{\(d\) proper \(r\)-clauses kill \(S\)}),
 \qquad
 R_g(S)=\mathbb P(\text{\(g\) proper \(k\)-clauses kill \(S\)}).$$ For a nonempty set, $q_0(S)=R_0(S)=0$. For the empty set these probabilities are one, including when there are zero clauses. Let $H_k(S)$ be the first killing index in an infinite sequence of proper $k$-clauses, counting the killing clause, and put $$H_k(\varnothing)=0,\qquad \tau_k(S)=\mathbb EH_k(S).$$ These expectations are finite: for every integer $m\ge0$, $$\label{eq:set-survival}
 \mathbb P(H_k(S)>m)
 \le |S|(1-2^{-k})^m
 \le 2^u\exp(-2^{-k}m).$$ Indeed, each fixed assignment survives each independent clause with probability $1-2^{-k}$, and the first inequality is a union bound.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Replacing one shorter clause

<a id="eq:frozen-killing"></a>
For a nonempty set $S$, a coordinate is *frozen* if every member of $S$ has the same value there. If $b$ coordinates are frozen, the probability that one proper $s$-clause kills $S$ is $$\label{eq:frozen-killing}
 h_s(S)=\frac{(b)_s}{2^s(u)_s},\qquad 1\le s\le u,$$ where $(a)_s=a(a-1)\cdots(a-s+1)$, with value zero for a nonnegative integer $a<s$. To kill $S$, every literal of the clause must be false for every assignment in $S$. Its variables must therefore be frozen, and each sign must oppose their common values. This characterizes the killing clauses and proves the formula.

<a id="eq:frozen-power"></a>
Write $h=h_r(S)$. Then $0\le h\le2^{-r}\le1/4$. If $b\ge k$, $$\label{eq:frozen-power}
 h_k(S)=h\frac{b-r}{2(u-r)}\ge\frac1k h^\beta.$$ In fact $b-r\ge b/k$, $u-r\le u$, and $$h=\frac1{2^r}\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\frac{b-j}{u-j}
 \le \left(\frac b{2u}\right)^r.$$ Substituting these bounds into the equality proves <a href="#eq:frozen-power" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frozen-power">[eq:frozen-power]</a>. Its hypothesis matters. For the cylinder whose first $r$ coordinates are fixed to zero, <a id="eq:frozen-exception"></a>$$\label{eq:frozen-exception}
 h_k(S)=0,\qquad h_r(S)=\frac1{2^r\binom ur}>0.$$ No single $k$-clause kills this set. A sufficiently long block can still do so through the joint effect of its clauses.

<a id="stmt-lem-one-replacement"></a>
<div id="lem:one-replacement" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (One-clause replacement). *For an integer $k\ge3$, let $$K_k=\bigl(2^k(k-1)\bigr)^{k-1}.$$ For every integer $u\ge k$, every $S\subseteq\{0,1\}^u$, and every integer $g\ge1$, <a id="eq:one-replacement"></a>$$\label{eq:one-replacement}
 R_g(S)\ge q_1(S)-K_k g^{-(k-1)}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-one-replacement"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-one-replacement</summary>

*Proof.* For $S=\varnothing$, both probabilities are one. Suppose $S$ is nonempty and retain $b,r,h$ above. A block kills $S$ if any one of its clauses does. Independence gives <a id="eq:individual-block-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:individual-block-bound}
 R_g(S)\ge1-(1-h_k(S))^g\ge1-e^{-g h_k(S)}.$$

First assume $b\ge k$. If $(g/k)h^{1/r}\ge2$, then <a href="#eq:frozen-power" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frozen-power">[eq:frozen-power]</a> gives $g h_k(S)\ge2h$, and $$R_g(S)\ge1-e^{-2h}\ge h.$$ For the last inequality, $1-e^{-2h}-h$ is zero at $h=0$ and has nonnegative derivative on $[0,1/4]$. If instead $(g/k)h^{1/r}<2$, then $$h<(2k)^r g^{-r}\le K_k g^{-r},$$ because $2k\le2^k(k-1)$. Nonnegativity of $R_g(S)$ now suffices.

If $b<r$, then $h=0$, which also proves the inequality. The only remaining case is $b=r$. Formula <a href="#eq:frozen-killing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frozen-killing">[eq:frozen-killing]</a> gives $$h=\frac1{2^r\binom ur}\le\left(\frac r{2u}\right)^r.$$ When $g<2^{k+1}u$, this is less than $(2^k r/g)^r=K_k g^{-r}$. When $g\ge2^{k+1}u$, apply <a href="#eq:set-survival" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:set-survival">[eq:set-survival]</a> to the full cube: $$R_g(S)\ge1-\exp((\log2-2)u)
 \ge1-e^{-u}>\frac14\ge h.$$ Here $u\ge k\ge3$. This last bound uses the whole block and remains valid even though $h_k(S)=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Several tests and the expected lifetime

Replacing tests one at a time requires a bound for arbitrary residual sets: conditioning on the other clauses need not leave a set with any special description. The sequential conditioning follows the replacement argument of Carenini (Carenini 2026b, Theorem 4.3) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Carenini](#ref-Carenini)]. The uniform additive block estimate in Lemma <a href="#lem:one-replacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:one-replacement">2</a> takes the place of the cutoff-dependent comparison of single-clause killing probabilities in (Carenini 2026b, Lemma 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Carenini](#ref-Carenini)]; the exceptional case $b=k-1$ above rules out a cutoff-free positive power lower bound for $h_k$ in terms of $h_r$.

<a id="stmt-lem-sequential-replacement"></a>
<div id="lem:sequential-replacement" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Sequential replacement). *For integers $k\ge3$ and $u\ge k$, every $S\subseteq\{0,1\}^u$, and all integers $d,g\ge1$, <a id="eq:sequential-replacement"></a>$$\label{eq:sequential-replacement}
 R_{dg}(S)\ge q_d(S)-dK_k g^{-(k-1)}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sequential-replacement"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sequential-replacement</summary>

*Proof.* Choose independent proper $r$-clauses $Q_1,\ldots,Q_d$ and independent blocks $G_1,\ldots,G_d$, each containing $g$ proper $k$-clauses; take all these choices mutually independent. Let $p_j$ be the probability that $G_1,\ldots,G_j,Q_{j+1},\ldots,Q_d$ kill $S$, for $0\le j\le d$.

For the replacement at index $j$, condition on $G_1,\ldots,G_{j-1},Q_{j+1},\ldots,Q_d$, and let $S'$ be the members of $S$ satisfying those clauses. The omitted $Q_j$ and fresh $G_j$ remain independent with their original laws. Their conditional killing probabilities are $q_1(S')$ and $R_g(S')$. Lemma <a href="#lem:one-replacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:one-replacement">2</a> applies also if $S'=\varnothing$, so $p_j\ge p_{j-1}-K_k g^{-r}$ after averaging. Summing from $j=1$ to $d$, using $p_0=q_d(S)$ and $p_d=R_{dg}(S)$, proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-lifetime"></a>
<div id="prop:lifetime" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:lifetime-product"></a>
**Proposition 4** (Lifetime from shorter-clause killing). *For an integer $k\ge3$, put $$\beta=\frac{k}{k-1},\qquad
 K_k=\bigl(2^k(k-1)\bigr)^{k-1},\qquad
 C_k=4(2K_k)^{1/(k-1)}.$$ For every integer $u\ge k$, every $S\subseteq\{0,1\}^u$, and every integer $d\ge1$, $$\label{eq:lifetime-product}
 \tau_k(S)q_d(S)^\beta\le C_k d^\beta.$$ Consequently, for nonempty $S$ and $0<a\le q_d(S)$, <a id="eq:lifetime-level"></a>$$\label{eq:lifetime-level}
 \tau_k(S)\le C_k d^\beta a^{-\beta}.$$ There is no upper restriction on $d$ and no positive lower cutoff on $a$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-lifetime"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-lifetime</summary>

*Proof.* For the empty set the lifetime is zero. If $S\ne\varnothing$ and $q_d(S)=0$, the left side of <a href="#eq:lifetime-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lifetime-product">[eq:lifetime-product]</a> is zero, since the lifetime is finite. Otherwise put $q=q_d(S)>0$ and choose $$g=\left\lceil\left(\frac{2K_kd}{q}\right)^{1/(k-1)}\right\rceil.$$ Sequential replacement gives $R_{dg}(S)\ge q/2$. Split an infinite independent sequence of $k$-clauses into blocks of length $dg$. The events that these blocks kill the *original* set $S$ are independent, each of probability at least $q/2$. The first successful block has expected index at most $2/q$, and the cumulative sequence kills $S$ no later than the end of that block. Hence $$\tau_k(S)\le\frac{2dg}{q}.$$ The quantity inside the ceiling is at least one, so $g$ is at most twice that quantity. It follows that $$\tau_k(S)
 \le4(2K_k)^{1/(k-1)}
       \left(\frac dq\right)^{1+1/(k-1)}
 =C_kd^\beta q^{-\beta}.$$ This proves the product bound and then the stated level bound. ◻

</details>

These inequalities also hold conditionally for a set $S$ measurable with respect to an exposed history, provided the comparison clauses remain independent of that history. Apply the deterministic-set inequality at each realization of $S$. In particular, sequential replacement conditions only on other independent clause positions; it does not condition the fresh clauses on a satisfiability event.

<a id="sec:deletion"></a>
### Clause omission and root tests

We now turn the lifetime estimate into a bound for the effect of deleting one clause. Its variables provide tests of the assignments that remain after deletion. We expose the incidence pattern through the whole cap, so that the number of tests is known, while keeping both their contents and the future ordinary clauses independent. The tests bound the probability of a deletion event; the ordinary clauses bound its remaining duration.

Fix an integer $k\ge3$, a real $B>0$, and $n\ge2k$. Put $L=\lfloor Bn\rfloor$, and for now suppose $L\ge1$. Let $C_1,C_2,\ldots$ be independent uniform proper $k$-clauses on $n$ variables, let $H$ be the first unsatisfiable prefix index, and put $T=\min\{H,L\}$. For $1\le i\le L$, define $$B_m^{(i)}=\bigwedge_{\substack{1\le j\le m\\j\ne i}}C_j,\qquad
 T^{(i)}=\min\bigl\{\inf\{m\ge0:B_m^{(i)}\notin\mathrm{SAT}\},L\bigr\},
 \qquad D_i=T^{(i)}-T.$$ The infimum is over integers and $\inf\varnothing=\infty$. Deleting $C_i$ retains all other clause indices. It can only delay unsatisfiability, so $D_i\ge0$; moreover $T^{(i)}$ depends only on the first $L$ clauses other than $C_i$.

<a id="stmt-lem-coordinate-omission"></a>
<div id="lem:coordinate-omission" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Coordinate omission). *For the independent clause process above, <a id="eq:coordinate-omission"></a>$$\label{eq:coordinate-omission}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(T)\le\sum_{i=1}^{L}\mathbb ED_i^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-coordinate-omission"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-coordinate-omission</summary>

*Proof.* This is the coordinate-omission form of the Efron–Stein method (Efron and Stein 1981) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[EfronStein](#ref-EfronStein)], recorded in (Boucheron et al. 2005, equation (3.6)) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BBLM](#ref-BBLM)]. Here is a direct martingale proof. Let $\mathcal H_j=\sigma(C_1,\ldots,C_j)$, with $\mathcal H_0$ trivial, and write $$M_i=\mathbb E[T\mid\mathcal H_i]-\mathbb E[T\mid\mathcal H_{i-1}].$$ Since $T^{(i)}$ omits the independent coordinate $C_i$, $\mathbb E[T^{(i)}\mid\mathcal H_i]
=\mathbb E[T^{(i)}\mid\mathcal H_{i-1}]$. Thus, with $X_i=T-T^{(i)}$, $$M_i=\mathbb E[X_i\mid\mathcal H_i]-\mathbb E[X_i\mid\mathcal H_{i-1}].$$ The tower property gives $$\mathbb EM_i^2
 =\mathbb E\bigl(\mathbb E[X_i\mid\mathcal H_i]\bigr)^2
  -\mathbb E\bigl(\mathbb E[X_i\mid\mathcal H_{i-1}]\bigr)^2
 \le\mathbb EX_i^2=\mathbb ED_i^2,$$ where the inequality uses conditional Jensen. The martingale differences are orthogonal and $T$ is $\mathcal H_L$-measurable, so summing proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### What the two filtrations reveal

Fix $i$ and condition on a value of $C_i$. Until the end of Section <a href="#sec:occupation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:occupation">4</a>, probabilities and expectations refer to this conditional law. The bounds will be uniform in the fixed clause. Its set $R$ of $k$ variables will be called the roots, and $t\in\{0,1\}^{R}$ is their unique assignment falsifying $C_i$. There are $u=n-k\ge k$ remaining variables. Write $B_m=B_m^{(i)}$.

<a id="eq:delay-identity"></a>
For $i\le m<L$, define the deletion event <a id="eq:deletion-events"></a>$$\label{eq:deletion-events}
 A_m=\{B_m\in\mathrm{SAT},\ B_m\wedge C_i\notin\mathrm{SAT}\}
     =\{T\le m<T^{(i)}\}.$$ On this event every solution of $B_m$ has root values $t$. The two prefix processes agree before $i$, and hence $$\label{eq:delay-identity}
 D_i=\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}\mathbf1_{A_m}.$$ In particular $D_L=0$: the displayed sum is then empty. The upper endpoint is $L-1$ because both failure times have already been stopped at $L$.

For $i\le m\le L$, define $S_m\subseteq\{0,1\}^{u}$ to be the nonroot restrictions of solutions of $B_m$ with root values $t$. The sets are defined on every outcome and decrease with $m$; for $m<L$, $A_m$ implies that $S_m$ is nonempty. We need to reveal this set while retaining independent randomness to test whether one of its assignments allows a root to be flipped.

The *root type* of a clause records the subset of its variables in $R$ and the signs of their literals. A clause is *ordinary* if that subset is empty. It is a *test clause* if the subset contains exactly one root and its literal is true at $t$. Let $O$ and $Q$ be the ordinary and test indices among $\{1,\ldots,L\}\setminus\{i\}$.

Reveal the root type at every such index through $L$. At indices in $\{1,\ldots,L\}\setminus(\{i\}\cup O\cup Q)$, also reveal the complete signed nonroot part. Let $\mathcal E$ be the sigma-field generated by this static information. Thus $O,Q$ and the counts <a id="eq:global-root-counts"></a>$$\label{eq:global-root-counts}
\begin{split}
 Z&=\#\{j\le L:j\ne i,\ C_j\text{ meets }R\},\\
 V&=\#\{j\le L:j\ne i,\ C_j\text{ meets at least two roots}\},
 \qquad d=\max\{1,Z\}
\end{split}$$ are $\mathcal E$-measurable. We call a clause counted by $V$ a *collision*. The ordinary clauses and the nonroot parts of test clauses have not yet been revealed.

<a id="stmt-lem-conditional-product"></a>
<div id="lem:conditional-product" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Conditional product law). *Conditional on $\mathcal E$, the clauses at indices in $O$ are independent uniform proper $k$-clauses on the $u$ nonroots. The nonroot parts at indices in $Q$ are independent uniform proper $(k-1)$-clauses on the nonroots. These two collections are mutually independent.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-conditional-product"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conditional-product</summary>

*Proof.* For a prescribed root type with $h$ roots and prescribed root signs, there are exactly $2^{k-h}\binom{u}{k-h}$ possible signed nonroot parts. Since complete proper $k$-clauses are equiprobable, these parts are equiprobable conditional on the type. Original clause independence makes this conditional law a product over the indices. Once the types are fixed, $O$ and $Q$ are fixed index sets. Revealing the parts outside $O\cup Q$ preserves the product law on the other indices. Their residual lengths are $k$ on $O$ and $k-1$ on $Q$, as asserted. ◻

</details>

A test clause is already true under $t$, so its nonroot part imposes no condition on $S_m$. Consequently $S_m$ is measurable with respect to $$\mathcal G_m
 =\sigma\bigl(\mathcal E,\ C_j:j\in O,\ j\le m\bigr).$$ We will bound the probability of $A_m$ under this filtration, which knows $S_m$ but leaves the test parts hidden. To bound the remaining duration on $A_m$, we also need to know whether $A_m$ has occurred. For that purpose, enlarge the filtration by revealing the past test parts: $$\mathcal F_m^*
 =\sigma\bigl(\mathcal G_m,\ 
       \text{nonroot parts of }C_j:j\in Q,\ j\le m\bigr).$$ The full baseline prefix $B_m$, and thus $A_m$, is $\mathcal F_m^*$-measurable. Under $\mathcal G_m$, the past test parts remain independent with their original conditional laws. Under either filtration, the future ordinary clauses remain independent uniform proper $k$-clauses. Both assertions follow from Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-product">6</a>. The static information includes future nonordinary data, but it includes no future ordinary contents.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Flipping a root gives an independent test

<a id="eq:available-root-tests"></a>
Recall that $q_d(S)$ is the probability that $d$ independent proper $(k-1)$-clauses kill $S$. Here $d=\max\{1,Z\}$ is already known under $\mathcal E$. Put $$\beta=\frac{k}{k-1},\qquad q_m=q_d(S_m).$$ For $j\in R$, let $\mathcal I_{j,m}$ be the test indices through $m$ whose root is $j$, and put $s_{j,m}=|\mathcal I_{j,m}|$. Then $s_{j,m}\le Z\le d$. Let $J_m\subseteq R$ consist of roots $j$ for which no collision through $m$ has exactly one root literal true under $t$, at root $j$. A collision can exclude at most one root, so $$\label{eq:available-root-tests}
 |J_m|\ge k-V.$$ The index sets and counts in this paragraph are $\mathcal E$-measurable.

<a id="stmt-lem-root-flip-tests"></a>
<div id="lem:root-flip-tests" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Root-flip tests). *For $i\le m<L$, on the event $\{|J_m|\ge1\}$, <a id="eq:root-flip-tests"></a>$$\label{eq:root-flip-tests}
 \mathbb P(A_m\mid\mathcal G_m)
 \le \mathbf1_{\{S_m\ne\varnothing\}}q_m^{|J_m|}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-root-flip-tests"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-root-flip-tests</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose $A_m$ occurs, and fix $j\in J_m$. The nonroot parts at the indices in $\mathcal I_{j,m}$ must kill $S_m$. If they did not, choose $x\in S_m$ satisfying all of them. Extend $x$ by the root assignment $t$, then flip root $j$. We check the clauses of $B_m$.

A clause with no true root literal under $t$ has a satisfied nonroot part, because $(t,x)$ satisfies $B_m$. The flip does not change that part. A clause with a true literal on another root remains true. Every remaining clause has $j$ as its unique true root under $t$. It cannot be a collision, by $j\in J_m$, so it is a test clause at an index in $\mathcal I_{j,m}$; its nonroot part is satisfied by the choice of $x$. The flipped assignment therefore satisfies $B_m$ with root values different from $t$, contradicting $A_m$.

Conditional on $\mathcal G_m$, the test parts belonging to distinct roots are disjoint independent families of proper $(k-1)$-clauses. For nonempty $S_m$, a family of $s_{j,m}\le d$ tests kills it with probability $q_{s_{j,m}}(S_m)\le q_d(S_m)$. This also covers $s_{j,m}=0$, whose killing probability is zero. The necessary tests for all roots in $J_m$ thus have conditional probability at most $q_m^{|J_m|}$. Finally $A_m$ requires $S_m\ne\varnothing$. ◻

</details>

The proof has kept the actual incidence count $d$, rather than discarding environments where it exceeds a chosen cutoff. We next bound duration with that same parameter; its moments will be averaged only after all conditional estimates have been proved.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### From ordinary clauses to chronological duration

For this comparison, keep the actual schedule and ordinary clauses through $L$. At every index after $L$, append an auxiliary independent uniform proper $k$-clause on the $u$ nonroots. The continuation is independent of the entire original process.

<a id="eq:chronological-domination"></a>
For $i\le m<L$ and $S_m\ne\varnothing$, let $K_m$ be the number of ordinary clauses strictly after $m$, including the first one that completes the killing of the original set $S_m$. Use the continuation if necessary. Let $U_m$ be the number of chronological indices after $m$ through that killing index. Set $K_m=U_m=0$ if $S_m=\varnothing$. The conditional product law and the independent continuation give <a id="eq:ordinary-future-mean"></a>$$\label{eq:ordinary-future-mean}
 \mathbb E[K_m\mid\mathcal F_m^*]
 =\mathbb E[K_m\mid\mathcal G_m]=\tau_k(S_m).$$ There are at most $Z$ nonordinary indices between $m$ and the kill: every one occurs before or at $L$, and the deleted index $i$ is not after $m$. Thus, pathwise, $$\label{eq:chronological-domination}
 U_m\le K_m+Z,\qquad
 \mathbb E[U_m\mid\mathcal F_m^*]\le\tau_k(S_m)+Z,\qquad
 \mathbb E[U_m\mid\mathcal G_m]\le\tau_k(S_m)+Z.$$ The continuation makes these statements valid even when no pre-cap ordinary clause completes the kill.

<a id="stmt-lem-weighted-delay"></a>
<div id="lem:weighted-delay" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:future-deletion-delay"></a>
<a id="eq:conditional-weighted-delay"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Weighted deletion delay). *Let $$W_m=\min\{L,\tau_k(S_m)+Z\}.$$ This is $\mathcal G_m$-measurable. For $i\le m<L$, on $A_m$, $$\label{eq:future-deletion-delay}
 \mathbb E\left[\sum_{\ell=m+1}^{L-1}\mathbf1_{A_\ell}
       \,\middle|\,\mathcal F_m^*\right]\le W_m.$$ Consequently $$\label{eq:conditional-weighted-delay}
 \mathbb E[D_i^2\mid\mathcal E]
 \le3\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}
        \mathbb E[\mathbf1_{A_m}W_m\mid\mathcal E].$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-weighted-delay"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-weighted-delay</summary>

*Proof.* On $A_m$, every later solution of $B_\ell$ must have root values $t$ and a nonroot restriction in $S_m$. Once the future ordinary clauses kill that original set, there can be no such solution, including at the killing index. If the kill is after $L$, the remaining capped interval ends first. In either case the number of later deletion events is at most $U_m$, and is also at most $L$. Taking conditional expectations and using <a href="#eq:chronological-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:chronological-domination">[eq:chronological-domination]</a> gives <a href="#eq:future-deletion-delay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:future-deletion-delay">[eq:future-deletion-delay]</a>.

On $A_m$, the set $S_m$ is nonempty and therefore needs at least one ordinary clause to be killed. Hence $\tau_k(S_m)\ge1$ and $W_m\ge1$. Expand the square of <a href="#eq:delay-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:delay-identity">[eq:delay-identity]</a>. For each cross term condition on the earlier $\mathcal F_m^*$; it contains $A_m$. The tower property gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[D_i^2\mid\mathcal E]
 &=\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}\mathbb E[\mathbf1_{A_m}\mid\mathcal E]\\
 &\quad+2\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}
  \mathbb E\left[\mathbf1_{A_m}
   \mathbb E\left[\sum_{\ell=m+1}^{L-1}\mathbf1_{A_\ell}
             \,\middle|\,\mathcal F_m^*\right]
             \,\middle|\,\mathcal E\right]\\
 &\le\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}
        \mathbb E[\mathbf1_{A_m}(1+2W_m)\mid\mathcal E]\\
 &\le3\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}
        \mathbb E[\mathbf1_{A_m}W_m\mid\mathcal E].\qedhere
\end{aligned}$$ ◻

</details>

Both the root-test probability and the remaining duration are now controlled by the same set $S_m$. We still have to bound how long that set can spend at each level of $q_d(S_m)$, and then average the incidence counts.

<a id="sec:occupation"></a>
### Occupation and incidence moments

Keep the deleted index $i$, its fixed clause $C_i$, and the environment $\mathcal E$ of Section <a href="#sec:deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:deletion">3</a>. We first bound the time spent at each level of the shorter-clause killing probability $q_m=q_d(S_m)$. We then combine that occupation bound with the root tests and average the incidence counts.

<a id="eq:weighted-lifetime-product"></a>
In this section $C_k$ denotes a positive constant depending only on the fixed $k$, which may increase between occurrences. It need not equal the explicit constant in Proposition <a href="#prop:lifetime" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lifetime">4</a>. Recall $\beta=k/(k-1)$, $d=\max\{1,Z\}$, and $W_m=\min\{L,\tau_k(S_m)+Z\}$. On $S_m\ne\varnothing$, $$\label{eq:weighted-lifetime-product}
 W_m q_m^\beta\le C_kd^\beta.$$ Indeed, Proposition <a href="#prop:lifetime" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lifetime">4</a> bounds $\tau_k(S_m)q_m^\beta$ by a constant times $d^\beta$, and $$Zq_m^\beta\le Z\le d\le d^\beta.$$ The product bound remains valid when $q_m=0$.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Time spent above a level

<a id="stmt-lem-conditional-occupation"></a>
<div id="lem:conditional-occupation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Conditional occupation). *There is a constant $C_k\ge1$, independent of the environment, such that for every $0<a\le1$, almost surely, <a id="eq:conditional-occupation"></a>$$\label{eq:conditional-occupation}
 \sum_{m=i}^{L-1}
 \mathbb P(S_m\ne\varnothing,\ q_m\ge a\mid\mathcal E)
 \le\min\{L,C_kd^\beta a^{-\beta}\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-conditional-occupation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conditional-occupation</summary>

*Proof.* Define $$N_a=\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}
       \mathbf1_{\{S_m\ne\varnothing,\ q_m\ge a\}},$$ and let $\sigma_a$ be the first index counted by $N_a$, or $\infty$ if there is none. Because $d$ is known in $\mathcal E$ and $S_m$ is $\mathcal G_m$-measurable, $\{\sigma_a=m\}\in\mathcal G_m$.

On $\{\sigma_a=m\}$, no eligible index precedes $m$. The future ordinary clauses used to define $U_m$ kill the original $S_m$ at index $m+U_m$. If that index is at most $L$, then $S_\ell$ is empty there and thereafter: a member would have to lie in $S_m$ and satisfy those same ordinary clauses. The eligible indices are therefore among $m,\ldots,m+U_m-1$. If the kill is after $L$, the capped interval ends earlier. Thus in both cases $$N_a\le U_m\quad\text{on }\{\sigma_a=m\}.$$ The first eligible index is included in these $U_m$ possible indices.

On $\{\sigma_a=m\}$, the lifetime bound and <a href="#eq:chronological-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:chronological-domination">[eq:chronological-domination]</a> give $$\mathbb E[U_m\mid\mathcal G_m]
 \le\tau_k(S_m)+Z
 \le C_kd^\beta a^{-\beta},$$ after increasing $C_k$, since $q_m\ge a$ and $Z\le d\le d^\beta a^{-\beta}$. The first-visit events are disjoint, so the tower property yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[N_a\mid\mathcal E]
 &\le\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}
   \mathbb E\left[\mathbf1_{\{\sigma_a=m\}}
                \mathbb E[U_m\mid\mathcal G_m]\mid\mathcal E\right]\\
 &\le C_kd^\beta a^{-\beta}
       \sum_{m=i}^{L-1}\mathbb P(\sigma_a=m\mid\mathcal E)
 \le C_kd^\beta a^{-\beta}.
\end{aligned}$$ Also $N_a\le L$. These two bounds prove the claim. ◻

</details>

Only the first visit to the level is stopped on. All later eligible indices are charged to the remaining lifetime of the same nested set. This is why the argument needs fresh future ordinary contents under $\mathcal G_m$, even though $\mathcal E$ has already exposed future nonordinary data.

To apply the occupation bound to the squared deletion delay, first consider an environment with $V=0$. All $k$ root tests are then available. Lemma <a href="#lem:root-flip-tests" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:root-flip-tests">7</a> supplies the factor $q_m^k$ in the weighted-delay bound <a href="#eq:conditional-weighted-delay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditional-weighted-delay">[eq:conditional-weighted-delay]</a>. On a nonempty $S_m$, <a href="#eq:weighted-lifetime-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-lifetime-product">[eq:weighted-lifetime-product]</a> gives $$W_mq_m^k=(W_mq_m^\beta)q_m^{k-\beta}
 \le C_kd^\beta q_m^{k-\beta}.$$ The remaining occupation sum has exponent $$\gamma=k-\beta=\frac{k(k-2)}{k-1}.$$ The lifetime estimate has controlled the remaining deletion duration; Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-occupation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-occupation">9</a> contributes a second lifetime factor $a^{-\beta}$ by bounding the time spent above level $a$. The resulting layer-cake integral contains $a^{\gamma-\beta-1}$. Its integrability at zero is determined by $\gamma-\beta=k(k-3)/(k-1)$, which is zero exactly at $k=3$.

<a id="stmt-lem-integrated-occupation"></a>
<div id="lem:integrated-occupation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Integrated occupation). *For $L\ge1$, almost surely and with a constant independent of the environment, <a id="eq:integrated-occupation"></a>$$\label{eq:integrated-occupation}
 \sum_{m=i}^{L-1}
 \mathbb E[\mathbf1_{\{S_m\ne\varnothing\}}q_m^\gamma\mid\mathcal E]
 \le
 \begin{cases}
 C_kd^\beta(1+\log L),&k=3,\\
 C_kd^\beta,&k\ge4.
 \end{cases}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-integrated-occupation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-integrated-occupation</summary>

*Proof.* Since $0\le q_m\le1$, the layer-cake identity and Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-occupation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-occupation">9</a> give <a id="eq:occupation-integral"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 &\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}
 \mathbb E[\mathbf1_{\{S_m\ne\varnothing\}}q_m^\gamma\mid\mathcal E]
 \notag\\
 &\quad=\int_0^1\gamma a^{\gamma-1}
       \sum_{m=i}^{L-1}
       \mathbb P(S_m\ne\varnothing,\ q_m\ge a\mid\mathcal E)\,da
 \notag\\
 &\quad\le\int_0^1\gamma a^{\gamma-1}
        \min\{L,Aa^{-\beta}\}\,da,\qquad
 A=C_kd^\beta\ge1.
 \label{eq:occupation-integral}
\end{aligned}$$ The terms are nonnegative, so the interchange of conditional expectation, the finite sum, and integration is valid.

For $k\ge4$, $\gamma>\beta$. Bounding the minimum by $Aa^{-\beta}$ gives $$\int_0^1\gamma a^{\gamma-1}\min\{L,Aa^{-\beta}\}\,da
 \le\frac{\gamma A}{\gamma-\beta}.$$ This is a constant times $A$ for fixed $k$.

For $k=3$, $\gamma=\beta=3/2$. If $A\ge L$, the integral is at most $L\le A$. If $A<L$, split at $a_*=(A/L)^{1/\beta}$. The lower part equals $La_*^\beta=A$; the upper part is $$\beta A\int_{a_*}^{1}\frac{da}{a}=A\log(L/A).$$ Thus the integral is at most $A[1+\log_+(L/A)]\le A(1+\log L)$, where $\log_+x=\max\{\log x,0\}$. This proves both cases. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### The cost of collisions

The number $V$ of collisions through the whole cap is fixed under $\mathcal E$. With no collisions all $k$ root tests are available. One collision may remove one test; two or more collisions will be handled by the deterministic cap.

<a id="stmt-prop-conditional-squared-delay"></a>
<div id="prop:conditional-squared-delay" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:conditional-squared-delay"></a>
**Proposition 11** (Conditional squared delay). *For $L\ge1$, set $$h_k(L)=
 \begin{cases}
 1+\log L,&k=3,\\
 1,&k\ge4.
 \end{cases}$$ Then, almost surely, $$\label{eq:conditional-squared-delay}
 \mathbb E[D_i^2\mid\mathcal E]
 \le C_kd^{2\beta}h_k(L)\mathbf1_{\{V=0\}}
      +C_kLd^\beta\mathbf1_{\{V=1\}}
      +L^2\mathbf1_{\{V\ge2\}}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-conditional-squared-delay"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-conditional-squared-delay</summary>

*Proof.* Because $W_m$ is $\mathcal G_m$-measurable, the tower property and the root-flip test bound imply, for every integer $1\le j\le k$ on the $\mathcal E$-measurable event $\{|J_m|\ge j\}$, $$\mathbb E[\mathbf1_{A_m}W_m\mid\mathcal E]
 \le\mathbb E[\mathbf1_{\{S_m\ne\varnothing\}}W_mq_m^j
         \mid\mathcal E].$$ Here we used $q_m\le1$ if more than $j$ tests are available.

If $V=0$, take $j=k$ and use $W_mq_m^k\le C_kd^\beta q_m^\gamma$ on nonempty sets, as above. Substituting in <a href="#eq:conditional-weighted-delay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditional-weighted-delay">[eq:conditional-weighted-delay]</a> and applying integrated occupation gives $C_kd^{2\beta}h_k(L)$.

If $V=1$, at least $k-1$ tests are available by <a href="#eq:available-root-tests" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:available-root-tests">[eq:available-root-tests]</a>. Since $k-1\ge\beta$ for $k\ge3$, $$\mathbf1_{\{S_m\ne\varnothing\}}W_mq_m^{k-1}
 \le C_kd^\beta.$$ Use this bound at each of the at most $L$ indices in <a href="#eq:conditional-weighted-delay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditional-weighted-delay">[eq:conditional-weighted-delay]</a>. It gives $C_kLd^\beta$. Finally, when $V\ge2$, the pathwise bound $0\le D_i\le L$ gives $D_i^2\le L^2$. This includes environments with no available test. ◻

</details>

The one-collision contribution contains $d^\beta$. Its average therefore needs the joint distribution of incidences and collisions, not just an upper bound on the probability of a collision.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Joint moments of incidences and collisions

<a id="stmt-lem-weighted-root-counts"></a>
<div id="lem:weighted-root-counts" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:incidence-moments"></a>
<a id="eq:weighted-collision-moments"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Weighted root counts). *Fix integers $k\ge3$ and $n\ge2k$, a real $B>0$, and a set of $k$ roots. Take $M$ independent uniform proper $k$-clauses on $n$ variables, where $M$ is a nonnegative integer with $M\le Bn$. Let $Z$ count clauses meeting a root, $V$ count clauses meeting at least two roots, and $d=\max\{1,Z\}$. For every fixed real $p\ge0$, $$\label{eq:incidence-moments}
 \mathbb Ed^p\le C_{k,B,p}.$$ For every fixed positive integer $r$, $$\label{eq:weighted-collision-moments}
 \mathbb E[d^p\mathbf1_{\{V\ge r\}}]\le C_{k,B,p,r}n^{-r}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-weighted-root-counts"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-weighted-root-counts</summary>

<a id="eq:one-trial-root-counts"></a>
*Proof.* For one clause, let $I$ indicate meeting a root and let $J$ indicate meeting at least two. Write $p_1=\mathbb P(I=1)$ and $p_2=\mathbb P(J=1)$. Union bounds over roots and pairs of roots give $$\label{eq:one-trial-root-counts}
 p_1\le\frac{k^2}{n},\qquad
 p_2\le\binom{k}{2}\frac{k(k-1)}{n(n-1)}
      \le\frac{k^2(k-1)^2}{n^2}.$$ Thus $Z\sim\operatorname{Bin}(M,p_1)$, and $$\mathbb Ee^Z=(1+p_1(e-1))^M
 \le\exp(Bk^2(e-1)).$$ For $K_p=\sup_{x\ge0}\max\{1,x\}^pe^{-x}<\infty$, we have $d^p\le K_pe^Z$. This proves <a href="#eq:incidence-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:incidence-moments">[eq:incidence-moments]</a>.

<a id="eq:joint-factorial-identity"></a>
For the collision bound, retain the dependence between $Z$ and $V$. Write $(a)_r=a(a-1)\cdots(a-r+1)$. For every nonnegative function $f$ on the nonnegative integers and $1\le r\le M$, $$\label{eq:joint-factorial-identity}
 \mathbb E[f(Z)(V)_r]
 =(M)_r p_2^r\,\mathbb Ef(r+X),
 \qquad X\sim\operatorname{Bin}(M-r,p_1).$$ To prove the identity, expand $(V)_r$ over ordered $r$-tuples of distinct clause indices. A collision at each chosen index has probability $p_2^r$ and forces its incidence indicator to be one. The remaining $M-r$ incidence indicators retain their independent Bernoulli laws with parameter $p_1$. Each ordered tuple has the same expectation, giving the formula.

Since $\mathbf1_{\{V\ge r\}}\le(V)_r/r!$, use $f(x)=\max\{1,x\}^p$ in <a href="#eq:joint-factorial-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:joint-factorial-identity">[eq:joint-factorial-identity]</a>: $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[d^p\mathbf1_{\{V\ge r\}}]
 &\le\frac{(M)_r p_2^r}{r!}\,
          \mathbb E\max\{1,r+X\}^p\\
 &\le\frac{(M)_r p_2^r}{r!}\,
          K_pe^r\exp(Bk^2(e-1))
 \le C_{k,B,p,r}n^{-r}.
\end{aligned}$$ The last inequality uses $M\le Bn$ and <a href="#eq:one-trial-root-counts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:one-trial-root-counts">[eq:one-trial-root-counts]</a>. If $r>M$, the left side is zero. The same definitions cover $M=0$ and $p=0$. ◻

</details>

These moments are taken with the deleted clause fixed and the other clauses still random. Once $\mathcal E$ is fixed, $Z$ and $V$ are known; their rarity returns only when we average over $\mathcal E$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Completing the capped upper bound

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-weighted-root-counts-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-weighted-root-counts</summary>

*Proof of the capped upper bound in Theorem <a href="#thm:variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:variance">1</a>.* Suppose first that $n\ge2k$ and $L\ge1$, and keep $C_i$ fixed. The remaining $L-1\le Bn$ clauses satisfy Lemma <a href="#lem:weighted-root-counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:weighted-root-counts">12</a>. Averaging <a href="#eq:conditional-squared-delay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditional-squared-delay">[eq:conditional-squared-delay]</a> over $\mathcal E$, and using the lemma with $p=2\beta$ in <a href="#eq:incidence-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:incidence-moments">[eq:incidence-moments]</a> and $(p,r)=(\beta,1),(0,2)$ in <a href="#eq:weighted-collision-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-collision-moments">[eq:weighted-collision-moments]</a>, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[D_i^2\mid C_i]
 &\le C_kh_k(L)\mathbb E[d^{2\beta}\mid C_i]
       +C_kL\mathbb E[d^\beta\mathbf1_{\{V=1\}}\mid C_i]
       +L^2\mathbb P(V\ge2\mid C_i)\\
 &\le C_{k,B}h_k(L)+C_{k,B}Ln^{-1}
                         +C_{k,B}L^2n^{-2}\\
 &\le C_{k,B}h_k(L).
\end{aligned}$$ The constants are uniform in $i$ and in the value of $C_i$. Remove this conditioning and use coordinate omission: $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(T)\le\sum_{i=1}^{L}\mathbb ED_i^2
 \le C_{k,B}Lh_k(L).$$ Since $L\le Bn$, this is at most $$C_{k,B}n\ell_k(n),\qquad
 \ell_k(n)=
 \begin{cases}
 \log(en),&k=3,\\
 1,&k\ge4.
 \end{cases}$$ For $k=3$, we used $1+\log L\le C_B\log(en)$ when $L\ge1$. If $L=0$, the capped variable is identically zero. The finitely many remaining sizes $k\le n<2k$ are covered by increasing the constant, since $0\le T\le\lfloor Bn\rfloor$. This proves the upper bound for every fixed positive $B$ and every $n\ge k$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:interfaces"></a>
### Caps, uncapped times, and finite-size means

The capped upper bound uses only finitely many clause coordinates. We now choose a sufficiently late cap and remove it, compare the first-unsatisfiable and last-satisfiable conventions with their exact endpoint corrections, and identify the limiting normalized means. The lower estimate, which requires a different input, is proved in Section <a href="#sec:lower-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:lower-bounds">6</a>.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Exact cap comparisons

<a id="eq:first-moment-tail"></a>
Fix $k\ge3$, put $q_k=1-2^{-k}$, and write $P_n(m)=\mathbb P(H_n>m)$. We also write $F_{n,m}=F_m$ when displaying the number of variables. Each assignment survives $m$ independent proper clauses with probability $q_k^m$. The expected number of surviving assignments therefore gives $$\label{eq:first-moment-tail}
 P_n(m)\le \min\{1,2^nq_k^m\}\qquad(m\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}).$$ In particular, $H_n$ is almost surely finite and has finite moments of every order. For integer caps $a\ge1$ and $b\ge0$, define $$X_a=\min\{H_n,a\},\qquad V_b=\min\{H_n-1,b\}.$$ These have the exact prefix identities <a id="eq:prefix-conventions"></a>$$\label{eq:prefix-conventions}
 P_n(m)=\mathbb P(X_a>m)\quad(0\le m<a),\qquad
 P_n(m)=\mathbb P(V_b\ge m)\quad(0\le m\le b).$$ All clause counts in these identities are integers.

<a id="stmt-prop-cap-bridge"></a>
<div id="prop:cap-bridge" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:cap-moments"></a>
<a id="eq:cap-sigma"></a>
<a id="eq:cap-variance"></a>
**Proposition 13** (Finite and infinite cap comparisons). *For $b\ge a$, put $R_{a,b}=V_b-X_a+1$ and $D_a=H_n-X_a$. Then <a id="eq:finite-cap-identity"></a><a id="eq:uncapped-identity"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 R_{a,b}&=\sum_{m=a}^{b}\mathbf 1_{\{H_n>m\}},
 &0\le R_{a,b}&\le(b-a+1)\mathbf 1_{\{H_n>a\}},\label{eq:finite-cap-identity}\\
 D_a&=(H_n-a)_+.
 \label{eq:uncapped-identity}
\end{aligned}$$ For either $Z=R_{a,b}$ or $Z=D_a$, writing $\xi_{n,a}=2^nq_k^a$, $$\label{eq:cap-moments}
 \mathbb EZ\le 2^k\xi_{n,a},\qquad
 \mathbb EZ^2\le (2^{2k+1}-2^k)\xi_{n,a}.$$ If $X=V_b$ or $X=H_n$, respectively, then $$\begin{aligned}
 0\le\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(X)}-\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(X_a)}
 &\le 2^k\sqrt{1+q_k}\,\xi_{n,a}^{1/2},
 \label{eq:cap-sigma}\\
 0\le\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(X)-\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(X_a)
 &\le\bigl[2a\,2^k+2^{2k+1}-2^k\bigr]\xi_{n,a}.
 \label{eq:cap-variance}
\end{aligned}$$ The mean identities are $\mathbb EV_b=\mathbb EX_a-1+\mathbb ER_{a,b}$ and $\mathbb EH_n=\mathbb EX_a+\mathbb ED_a$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-cap-bridge"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-cap-bridge</summary>

*Proof.* The finite identity follows by considering $H_n\le a$ and $H_n>a$; it also gives $R_{a,b}=\min\{(H_n-a)_+,b-a+1\}$. For every integer $r\ge0$, $$\mathbb P(D_a>r)=P_n(a+r)\le\xi_{n,a}q_k^r.$$ The integer tail-sum identities yield $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb ED_a&=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}P_n(a+r)
       \le\frac{\xi_{n,a}}{1-q_k},\\
 \mathbb ED_a^2&=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}(2r+1)P_n(a+r)
       \le\frac{1+q_k}{(1-q_k)^2}\xi_{n,a}.
\end{aligned}$$ For $R_{a,b}$ the same sums end at $r=b-a$, so the bounds also apply. Since $X_aZ=aZ$ for either remainder, $$\operatorname{Cov}(X_a,Z)=(a-\mathbb EX_a)\mathbb EZ\ge0.$$ The constant shift in the finite-cap identity does not affect variance. Thus $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(X)-\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(X_a)
 =2(a-\mathbb EX_a)\mathbb EZ+\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(Z)\ge0.$$ Using <a href="#eq:cap-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cap-moments">[eq:cap-moments]</a> proves <a href="#eq:cap-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cap-variance">[eq:cap-variance]</a>. The triangle inequality for centered variables in $L^2$ gives $$\bigl|\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(X)}-\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(X_a)}\bigr|
 \le\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(Z)}\le\sqrt{\mathbb EZ^2},$$ which proves <a href="#eq:cap-sigma" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cap-sigma">[eq:cap-sigma]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:cap-rounding"></a>
Recall the first-moment cap density <a id="eq:sufficient-cap"></a>$$\label{eq:sufficient-cap}
 U_k=\frac{\log2}{-\log q_k}.$$ For any fixed $B>U_k$, let $\rho_{k,B}=2q_k^B<1$. The cap $a=\lfloor Bn\rfloor$ satisfies $$\label{eq:cap-rounding}
 \xi_{n,a}=q_k^{\lfloor Bn\rfloor-Bn}\rho_{k,B}^n
 \le q_k^{-1}\rho_{k,B}^n.$$ The factor $q_k^{-1}$ accounts for the floor; a ceiling cap needs no such factor. Equality $B=U_k$ does not give exponential decay by this argument. In particular, $B=2^{k+1}$ is sufficient for every fixed $k\ge3$.

<a id="stmt-cor-uncapped"></a>
<div id="cor:uncapped" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:mean-centered-concentration"></a>
**Corollary 14** (Uncapped variance and finite-size concentration). *For independent uniform proper clauses with fixed $k\ge3$, <a id="eq:uncapped-variance"></a>$$\label{eq:uncapped-variance}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(H_n)=
 \begin{cases}
 O(n\log n),&k=3,\\
 O_k(n),&k\ge4.
 \end{cases}$$ The same upper bounds hold for the uncapped last satisfiable index $H_n-1$ and, for every fixed $B>0$, for $\min\{H_n-1,\lfloor Bn\rfloor\}$. Write $s_k(n)=\sqrt{n\ell_k(n)}$, with $\ell_k$ as in the introduction. For each of these statistics, or for $T_{n,B}$ from Theorem <a href="#thm:variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:variance">1</a>, there is a constant $C$ (depending on fixed $k$ and, when present, $B$) such that $$\label{eq:mean-centered-concentration}
 \mathbb P\bigl(|X-\mathbb EX|\ge t s_k(n)\bigr)\le C t^{-2}
 \qquad(t>0)$$ for all sufficiently large $n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-uncapped"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-uncapped</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a fixed $B>U_k$ and use the capped upper bound of Theorem <a href="#thm:variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:variance">1</a> for $X_a=T_{n,B}$, $a=\lfloor Bn\rfloor$. Equations <a href="#eq:cap-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cap-variance">[eq:cap-variance]</a> and <a href="#eq:cap-rounding" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cap-rounding">[eq:cap-rounding]</a> transfer its bound to $H_n$, with an additive $O_{k,B}(n\rho_{k,B}^n)$ error. Subtracting one leaves variance unchanged. For any real random variable $Y$ with finite variance, any independent copy $Y'$, and any $1$-Lipschitz function $f$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(f(Y))=\tfrac12\mathbb E(f(Y)-f(Y'))^2
 \le\tfrac12\mathbb E(Y-Y')^2=\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(Y).$$ Apply this to $f(y)=\min\{y,\lfloor Bn\rfloor\}$ and $Y=H_n-1$. Finally, Chebyshev’s inequality gives <a href="#eq:mean-centered-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mean-centered-concentration">[eq:mean-centered-concentration]</a>. Increasing the upper-bound constants covers the finitely many remaining $n\ge k$, since all uncapped moments are finite. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:normalized-mean-bridge"></a>
The mean correction is more precise than its normalized limiting effect: for $a=\lfloor Bn\rfloor$ with $B>U_k$, $$\label{eq:normalized-mean-bridge}
 0\le\frac{\mathbb EH_n-\mathbb ET_{n,B}}n
 \le\frac{2^kq_k^{-1}}n\rho_{k,B}^n.$$ Theorem 1.1 of (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[FixedKThreshold](#ref-FixedKThreshold)] gives a unique positive finite threshold $\alpha_k$ in exactly this proper-clause model. It also implies <a id="eq:mean-limit"></a>$$\label{eq:mean-limit}
 \frac{\mathbb EH_n}{n}\longrightarrow\alpha_k,
 \qquad
 \frac{\mathbb ET_{n,B}}n\longrightarrow\alpha_k
 \quad(B>U_k).$$ Here is the explicit expectation argument. Take $b=2^{k+1}n$. The theorem and monotonicity imply $\min\{H_n-1,b\}/n\to\alpha_k$ in probability; the first-moment bound implies $\alpha_k\le U_k<2^{k+1}$. The bounded statistic therefore converges in mean. Proposition <a href="#prop:cap-bridge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cap-bridge">13</a> first compares it with $X_b$, then with $H_n$; the errors after the one-index shift are exponentially small. Equation <a href="#eq:normalized-mean-bridge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-mean-bridge">[eq:normalized-mean-bridge]</a> handles the remaining caps. The same conclusion holds for last-satisfiable caps of density $B>U_k$. These limits give no rate for $\mathbb EH_n-\alpha_k n$ or for the corresponding capped bias. The concentration in <a href="#eq:mean-centered-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mean-centered-concentration">[eq:mean-centered-concentration]</a> is centered at each finite-size expectation.

<a id="sec:lower-bounds"></a>
### Quantile separation and the variance lower bound

The remaining part of Theorem <a href="#thm:variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:variance">1</a> is a linear lower bound. We use a width theorem for the proper-clause model and convert it into a second-moment estimate. Keeping the two integer quantiles explicit makes the argument valid when the hitting-time law has atoms.

An elementary sparse-prefix estimate will also supply the positive lower bounds on finite-size means in Appendices <a href="#sec:direct-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:direct-comparison">9</a> and <a href="#sec:appendix-c" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:appendix-c">10</a>. We record it before turning to the quantile argument.

<a id="stmt-lem-sparse-satisfiability"></a>
<div id="lem:sparse-satisfiability" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (A sparse satisfiable prefix). *For every real $0<c<e^{-2}$, every integer $k\ge3$, and every $n\ge k$, put $j=\lfloor cn\rfloor$. In the independent proper-clause model, <a id="eq:sparse-satisfiability"></a>$$\label{eq:sparse-satisfiability}
 1-P_n(j)\le\frac{ec}{n^2}\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}t^2(e^2c)^t=O_c(n^{-2}).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sparse-satisfiability"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sparse-satisfiability</summary>

*Proof.* Form the bipartite incidence graph between the $j$ clause positions and the $n$ variables. A matching covering all clause positions supplies a satisfying assignment: set each matched variable to satisfy its literal in the clause matched to it. This criterion and the deficient-set union bound below appear in Franco and Van Gelder (Franco and Van Gelder 2003, sec. 8) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[FrancoVanGelder2003](#ref-FrancoVanGelder2003)]. By Hall’s theorem (Hall 1935, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Hall1935](#ref-Hall1935)], failure of such a matching implies that some set of $t$ variables contains at least $t+1$ whole clauses. A proper $k$-clause lies in a specified $t$-set with probability $(t)_k/(n)_k\le(t/n)^k$, interpreted as zero when $t<k$. Thus, using $k\ge3$, $j\le cn$, and $\binom ab\le(ea/b)^b$, $$\begin{aligned}
 1-P_n(j)
 &\le\sum_{\substack{1\le t\le n\\t+1\le j}}
       \binom nt\binom j{t+1}(t/n)^{k(t+1)}\\
 &\le\sum_{t=1}^{n}ec(e^2c)^t(t/n)^{t+2}
 \le\frac{ec}{n^2}\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}t^2(e^2c)^t.
\end{aligned}$$ The final series is finite because $e^2c<1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:wilson-gap"></a>
Wilson’s Corollary 4 (Wilson 2002) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Wilson](#ref-Wilson)] applies to independent uniform proper $k$-clauses sampled with replacement. At the fixed probability levels $3/4$ and $1/4$, its assertion for every fixed $k\ge3$ gives constants $d_k>0$ and $n_k$ such that, for $n\ge n_k$, $$\label{eq:wilson-gap}
 P_n(m_1)\ge\tfrac34,\quad P_n(m_2)\le\tfrac14
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad m_2-m_1\ge d_k\sqrt n$$ The constants may depend on $k$. We spell out the conversion from this quantile-width statement to a variance bound, including possible atoms.

<a id="stmt-prop-variance-lower"></a>
<div id="prop:variance-lower" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16** (Linear variance lower bound). *Let $L_n$ be positive integer caps of order $O(n)$ and suppose $P_n(L_n-1)\le1/4$ for all sufficiently large $n$. Then $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\min\{H_n,L_n\})\ge c_k n$$ for all sufficiently large $n$, with $c_k>0$. In particular, for every fixed $B>U_k$ and every fixed $k\ge4$, <a id="eq:sharp-variance"></a>$$\label{eq:sharp-variance}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(H_n)=\Theta_k(n),\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(T_{n,B})=\Theta_{k,B}(n),\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\min\{H_n-1,\lfloor Bn\rfloor\})=\Theta_{k,B}(n).$$ For $k=3$, the corresponding variances lie between positive constant multiples of $n$ and $n\log n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-variance-lower"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-variance-lower</summary>

*Proof.* Write $X=\min\{H_n,L_n\}$ and $G(j)=\mathbb P(X\le j)$. Define the integer quantiles $$a=\min\{j:G(j)\ge1/4\},\qquad
 b=\min\{j:G(j)\ge3/4\}.$$ The cap hypothesis gives $b\le L_n-1$. Thus $a-1$ and $b$ are below the cap and, by minimality, $$P_n(a-1)=1-G(a-1)>3/4,\qquad P_n(b)=1-G(b)\le1/4.$$ Applying <a href="#eq:wilson-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wilson-gap">[eq:wilson-gap]</a> to the endpoint probabilities above gives $b-a\ge d_k\sqrt n-1$. Minimality of the quantiles also gives $$\mathbb P(X\le a)\ge\tfrac14,\qquad
 \mathbb P(X\ge b)=1-G(b-1)>\tfrac14.$$ For an independent copy $X'$, each of the disjoint events $\{X\le a,X'\ge b\}$ and $\{X'\le a,X\ge b\}$ has probability at least $1/16$ once $b>a$. Therefore $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(X)=\tfrac12\mathbb E(X-X')^2
 \ge\frac{(b-a)^2}{16}
 \ge\frac{d_k^2}{64}n$$ for sufficiently large $n$. If $L_n=\lfloor Bn\rfloor$ and $B>U_k$, then $$P_n(L_n-1)\le q_k^{-2}\rho_{k,B}^n=o(1)$$ by <a href="#eq:first-moment-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:first-moment-tail">[eq:first-moment-tail]</a>. The capped upper bounds proved above give the matching upper bounds. The exact identity $$\min\{H_n-1,L_n\}=\min\{H_n,L_n+1\}-1$$ proves the lower bound for last satisfiability, since its required condition is $P_n(L_n)\le1/4$. Finally $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(H_n)\ge\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\min\{H_n,L_n\})$ by Proposition <a href="#prop:cap-bridge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cap-bridge">13</a>; Corollary <a href="#cor:uncapped" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:uncapped">14</a> supplies the uncapped upper bound. This completes the variance assertions of Theorem <a href="#thm:variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:variance">1</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:three-clause-refinements"></a>
### Three-clause cap and Poisson refinements

<a id="eq:three-same-cap"></a>
<a id="eq:three-cross-cap"></a>
<a id="eq:three-center-upper"></a>
At clause size three, the cap identities connect the first-unsatisfiable statistic used in the variance proof to the last-satisfiable statistics used in the threshold companion (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[FixedKThreshold](#ref-FixedKThreshold)]. We give their exact constants, then transfer concentration to the companion’s auxiliary Poisson clause law, which allows repeated variables within a clause. The Poisson density interval and the cap on the hitting time play different roles and can be chosen independently. For $k=3$, define the three distinct statistics $$T=\min\{H_n,10n\},\qquad
 W=\min\{H_n-1,10n\},\qquad
 V=\min\{H_n-1,16n\}.$$ With $\rho=2(7/8)^{10}<1$, Proposition <a href="#prop:cap-bridge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cap-bridge">13</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 W&=T-1+\mathbf 1_{\{F_{n,10n}\in\mathrm{SAT}\}},\label{eq:three-same-cap}\\
 V&=T-1+R,\qquad
 0\le R\le(6n+1)\mathbf 1_{\{F_{n,10n}\in\mathrm{SAT}\}}.
 \label{eq:three-cross-cap}
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently $0\le\mathbb EV-(\mathbb ET-1)\le(6n+1)\rho^n$ and $|\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(V)}-\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(T)}|\le(6n+1)\rho^{n/2}$. The sharper geometric bounds proved above are $$\mathbb ER\le8\rho^n,\qquad \mathbb ER^2\le120\rho^n,\qquad
 0\le\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(V)-\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(T)\le(160n+120)\rho^n.$$ The pathwise inequality $W\le6n+4n\mathbf 1_{\{H_n>6n\}}$ and the density-six first moment, with $\sigma=2(7/8)^6<1$, give $$\label{eq:three-center-upper}
 \frac{\mathbb EW}{n}\le6+4\sigma^n=6+o(1).$$ The survival-sum bound $\mathbb EH_n\le6n+8\sigma^n$ proves the same $6+o(1)$ upper bound for $T$ and $V$. Their normalized expectations are therefore all at most $7$ for sufficiently large $n$.

<a id="eq:three-poisson-sandwich"></a>
On the density interval $0\le c\le8$, the three-clause Poisson law gives the following explicit transfer constants. Let $s_n(c)$ be the satisfiability probability for $\operatorname{Pois}(cn)$ clauses, where the three variable choices within each clause are independent uniform choices with replacement and the signs are independent and fair. Internal repetitions and tautologies are allowed in this auxiliary law. The distinct-variable fraction is exactly $$\vartheta_n=\frac{(n)_3}{n^3}=1-\frac3n+\frac2{n^2}.$$ Poisson thinning gives independent proper and repeated-variable clause collections. The proper count $J$ has distribution $\operatorname{Pois}(cn\vartheta_n)$, while the other count has mean $$cn(1-\vartheta_n)=c(3-2/n)\le24\qquad(0\le c\le8).$$ Requiring no repeated-variable clauses for a lower bound, and deleting them for an upper bound, gives $$\label{eq:three-poisson-sandwich}
 e^{-24}\mathbb EP_n(J)\le s_n(c)\le\mathbb EP_n(J),\qquad
 cn-24\le\mathbb EJ\le cn,\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(J)\le8n.$$ Set $\omega_n=\mathbb EW/n$. The variance upper bound implies, in particular, $\mathbb P(|W-n\omega_n|\ge n^{11/12})=O(n^{-1/6})$. Chebyshev’s inequality for $J$ and <a href="#eq:three-poisson-sandwich" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:three-poisson-sandwich">[eq:three-poisson-sandwich]</a> therefore yield, uniformly for $c\in[0,8]$, <a id="eq:three-poisson-lower"></a><a id="eq:three-poisson-upper"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 c\le\omega_n-2n^{-1/12}
 &\quad\Longrightarrow\quad s_n(c)\ge e^{-24}/2,
 \label{eq:three-poisson-lower}\\
 c\ge\omega_n+2n^{-1/12}
 &\quad\Longrightarrow\quad s_n(c)=O(n^{-1/6}).
 \label{eq:three-poisson-upper}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed, in the lower case $\mathbb EJ\le n\omega_n-2n^{11/12}$, so $J\le n\omega_n-n^{11/12}$ except with probability $O(n^{-5/6})$. In the upper case $\mathbb EJ\ge n\omega_n+2n^{11/12}-24$, and the opposite deviation has the same bound. Apply concentration at the indicated integer counts; for counts beyond $10n$, use monotonicity at $\lceil n\omega_n+n^{11/12}\rceil<10n$, valid by <a href="#eq:three-center-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:three-center-upper">[eq:three-center-upper]</a>. These statements use the last-SAT cap $10n$ and Poisson density ceiling $8$, giving the lower constant $e^{-24}/2$. The general transfer lemma of the threshold companion permits a cap density $\lambda$ and a separate Poisson density ceiling $D$, each above a fixed upper bound for the normalized centers (OpenAI 2026a, Lemma 5.3) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[FixedKThreshold](#ref-FixedKThreshold)]. Specializing it to $k=3$ and $\lambda=D=16$ uses the last-SAT statistic $V$, center $\mathbb EV/n$, and interval $c\in[0,16]$. In that specialization the repetition bound is $48$ and the lower constant is $e^{-48}/2$.

<a id="sec:replacement-sharpness"></a>
### Sharpness for arbitrary assignment sets

For each fixed integer $k\ge3$, two exponents in Section <a href="#sec:replacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:replacement">2</a> are optimal for estimates uniform over arbitrary assignment sets: the power $k/(k-1)$ of the inverse killing probability in the lifetime bound, and the power $k-1$ of the inverse block length in the one-clause replacement error. Both examples below use subcubes with many frozen coordinates. A common observation identifies when a block of clauses can kill such a subcube.

Write $r=k-1$. We retain the proper clause law: a proper $s$-clause on $u$ coordinates chooses $s$ distinct coordinates uniformly and gives them independent fair signs. Different clauses are independent, with repetition of whole clauses allowed. Recall that $q_1(S)$ is the probability that one proper $r$-clause kills $S$, $R_t(S)$ is the probability that $t$ proper $k$-clauses kill $S$, and $\tau_k(S)=\mathbb EH_k(S)$. For nonempty $S$, $H_k(S)$ is the least positive index $t$ for which the first $t$ proper $k$-clauses jointly kill $S$; the convention for the empty set is $H_k(\varnothing)=0$.

<a id="eq:sharp-subcube-probabilities"></a>
<a id="eq:sharp-successive-ratio"></a>
For integers $u\ge b\ge k$, consider the nonempty subcube $$S_{u,b}=\{x\in\{0,1\}^u:x_1=\cdots=x_b=0\}.$$ Its first $b$ coordinates are frozen and the others are free. If $h_s(S_{u,b})$ denotes the probability that one proper $s$-clause kills this subcube, the frozen-coordinate formula <a href="#eq:frozen-killing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frozen-killing">[eq:frozen-killing]</a> reads $$\label{eq:sharp-subcube-probabilities}
 h_s(S_{u,b})=\frac{(b)_s}{2^s(u)_s}\quad(1\le s\le u),
 \qquad q_1(S_{u,b})=h_r(S_{u,b}),$$ where $(a)_s=a(a-1)\cdots(a-s+1)$. In particular, $$\label{eq:sharp-successive-ratio}
 \frac{h_k(S_{u,b})}{q_1(S_{u,b})}
 =\frac{b-r}{2(u-r)}.$$

<a id="eq:sharp-subcube-bounds"></a>
For every integer $t\ge1$, the same subcube satisfies $$\label{eq:sharp-subcube-bounds}
 1-\bigl(1-h_k(S_{u,b})\bigr)^t
 \le R_t(S_{u,b})
 \le t h_k(S_{u,b})+\binom t2\frac{k^2}{u}.$$ The lower bound records the independent chances that a single clause kills the whole subcube. To prove the upper bound, restrict all $t$ clauses to the frozen values. Each restricted clause is already true, is the empty false clause, or is a nonempty clause on free coordinates. If no restricted clause is empty and false, and no two original clauses share a free coordinate, then the remaining nonempty clauses use disjoint sets of variables. Choose one literal in each and set it true, then assign the other free coordinates arbitrarily. The resulting assignment lies in $S_{u,b}$ and satisfies the block. Thus a block can kill the subcube only if an individually killing clause occurs or a pair of clauses shares a free coordinate. For a fixed free coordinate, the probability that both independent proper clauses contain it is $(k/u)^2$. Summing over the $u-b$ free coordinates bounds the probability for a given pair by $k^2/u$. A union bound over clauses and pairs proves the upper bound in <a href="#eq:sharp-subcube-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-subcube-bounds">[eq:sharp-subcube-bounds]</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-lifetime-sharp"></a>
<div id="prop:lifetime-sharp" class="proposition">

**Proposition 17** (Optimality of the lifetime exponent). *Fix an integer $k\ge3$, and put $\beta=k/(k-1)$. For every real $\gamma<\beta$ and all constants $D,A>0$, there exist arbitrarily large integers $u\ge k$ and nonempty sets $S\subseteq\{0,1\}^u$ such that $$q_1(S)\ge A u^{-(k-1)},\qquad
 \tau_k(S)>Dq_1(S)^{-\gamma}.$$ Consequently, the killing-probability exponent $\beta$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:lifetime" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lifetime">4</a> cannot be decreased in a bound uniform over arbitrary assignment sets, even above any fixed multiple of the scale $u^{-(k-1)}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-lifetime-sharp"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-lifetime-sharp</summary>

*Proof.* Let the integer $N\ge2$ tend to infinity, and put $$u=N^{2k+1},\qquad b=N^{2k},\qquad S=S_{u,b}.$$ Since $k$ is fixed and $b/u=N^{-1}$, <a href="#eq:sharp-subcube-probabilities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-subcube-probabilities">[eq:sharp-subcube-probabilities]</a> gives <a id="eq:sharp-frozen-probabilities"></a>$$\label{eq:sharp-frozen-probabilities}
 a_N:=q_1(S)\sim 2^{-r}N^{-r},
 \qquad h_N:=h_k(S)\sim 2^{-k}N^{-k}.$$ Choose $t_N=\lfloor1/(4h_N)\rfloor$. This is positive for all sufficiently large $N$, and $t_N=\Theta_k(N^k)$. A set survives the first $t_N$ clauses exactly when $H_k(S)>t_N$, so <a href="#eq:sharp-subcube-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-subcube-bounds">[eq:sharp-subcube-bounds]</a> yields <a id="eq:sharp-survival"></a>$$\label{eq:sharp-survival}
 \mathbb P(H_k(S)>t_N)
 =1-R_{t_N}(S)
 \ge 1-t_Nh_N-\binom{t_N}{2}\frac{k^2}{u}
 \ge\frac34-o(1).$$ Here $t_Nh_N\le1/4$, while $t_N^2/u=O_k(N^{-1})$. It follows that $$\tau_k(S)\ge t_N\mathbb P(H_k(S)>t_N)=\Omega_k(N^k).$$ For the reverse bound, let $J$ be the index of the first clause that kills the original set $S$ by itself. The cumulative sequence kills $S$ no later than $J$. Since $J$ has the geometric distribution with success probability $h_N>0$, $$\tau_k(S)\le\mathbb EJ=h_N^{-1}=O_k(N^k).$$ Thus $$\tau_k(S)=\Theta_k(N^k)
          =\Theta_k\bigl(a_N^{-\beta}\bigr).$$ For each fixed $\gamma<\beta$, the ratio $\tau_k(S)/a_N^{-\gamma}$ tends to infinity, since $k-r\gamma>0$. Finally, $$a_Nu^r\sim 2^{-r}N^{2kr}=2^{-r}b^r\longrightarrow\infty.$$ For all sufficiently large $N$, the two displayed requirements therefore hold for the prescribed $D$ and $A$. The values of $u$ are arbitrarily large. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-replacement-sharp"></a>
<div id="prop:replacement-sharp" class="proposition">

**Proposition 18** (Optimality of the block-error exponent). *Fix an integer $k\ge3$. For integers $g\ge2$, there are nonempty sets $S_g\subseteq\{0,1\}^{u_g}$, with $u_g\ge k$, for which $$q_1(S_g)-R_g(S_g)\sim 2^{-k}g^{-(k-1)}
 \qquad(g\to\infty).$$ In particular, no error $o(g^{-(k-1)})$, uniform over $u$ and the assignment set, can replace the error in Lemma <a href="#lem:one-replacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:one-replacement">2</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-replacement-sharp"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-replacement-sharp</summary>

*Proof.* For every integer $g\ge2$, put $$u_g=g^{k+2},\qquad b_g=g^{k+1},\qquad S_g=S_{u_g,b_g},$$ and abbreviate $a_g=q_1(S_g)$ and $h_g=h_k(S_g)$. Equations <a href="#eq:sharp-subcube-probabilities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-subcube-probabilities">[eq:sharp-subcube-probabilities]</a> and <a href="#eq:sharp-successive-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-successive-ratio">[eq:sharp-successive-ratio]</a> give $$a_g\sim2^{-r}g^{-r},\qquad
 \frac{gh_g}{a_g}=\frac{g(b_g-r)}{2(u_g-r)}
 \longrightarrow\frac12.$$ The upper bound in <a href="#eq:sharp-subcube-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-subcube-bounds">[eq:sharp-subcube-bounds]</a>, and the two-term inclusion–exclusion lower bound for the independent individual killing events, give $$gh_g-\binom g2h_g^2
 \le R_g(S_g)
 \le gh_g+\binom g2\frac{k^2}{u_g}.$$ The collision error is $O_k(g^{-k})=o(a_g)$. Also $h_g=\Theta_k(g^{-k})$, so the inclusion–exclusion error is $O_k(g^{-2(k-1)})=o(a_g)$. Hence $$R_g(S_g)=gh_g+o(a_g),\qquad
 q_1(S_g)-R_g(S_g)
   =\bigl(\tfrac12+o(1)\bigr)a_g
   \sim2^{-k}g^{-r}.$$ This is the stated asymptotic. ◻

</details>

These constructions delimit estimates uniform over arbitrary assignment sets. They leave open stronger lifetime estimates for classes of sets with additional structure, and variance arguments using information beyond the killing probability. In particular, they do not show that the logarithm in the three-clause variance upper bound is necessary.

<a id="sec:direct-comparison"></a>
### Comparing finite-size centers with proper clauses

The variance estimate controls fluctuations at one size. We now compare the corresponding centers at different sizes, using proper clauses at every step. The comparison keeps the weights of the two variable blocks; this is what makes its error uniform even when one block is much larger than the other. Once these probability comparisons are established, the deterministic balanced-comparison lemma of the threshold companion turns them into convergence. This gives a second route to convergence from a concentration estimate, without using the companion’s limiting-threshold theorem as Section <a href="#sec:interfaces" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:interfaces">5</a> does. The theorem below takes concentration as an input, so it also applies to the independent, weaker variance proof in Appendix <a href="#sec:appendix-c" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:appendix-c">10</a>.

Interpolation between a system and independent parts appears in the thermodynamic-limit work of Guerra and Toninelli (Guerra and Toninelli 2002) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[GuerraToninelli](#ref-GuerraToninelli)] and the diluted-system work of Franz and Leone (Franz and Leone 2003) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[FranzLeone](#ref-FranzLeone)] and Franz, Leone, and Toninelli (Franz, Leone, and Toninelli 2003) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[FranzLeoneToninelli](#ref-FranzLeoneToninelli)]. Those works concern free energies under their respective model hypotheses; they provide methodological context for the size comparison. The one-clause interpolation here uses frozen variables, as in the satisfiability interpolation of Bayati, Gamarnik, and Tetali (Bayati, Gamarnik, and Tetali 2013, sec. 4, Proposition 2) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[BGT](#ref-BGT)]. Their clause law samples variable tuples with replacement. Here the clauses have distinct variables, so the exact killing probabilities contain falling factorials. We control the resulting error locally before summing it over the trials.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### A center theorem with an explicit concentration input

<a id="cmp:prefix-endpoint"></a>
For fixed $k\ge3$ and $n\ge k$, let $H_n$ be the index of the first unsatisfiable prefix of independent uniform proper $k$-clauses on $n$ variables, and write $P_n(j)=\mathbb P(H_n>j)$ for integers $j\ge0$. Fix a real $B>0$ and set $$L_n=\lfloor Bn\rfloor,\qquad T_n=\min\{H_n,L_n\},\qquad
 \mu_n=\mathbb ET_n,\qquad e_n=\mu_n/n.$$ The endpoint for this first-unsatisfiable convention is strict: $$\label{cmp:prefix-endpoint}
 P_n(j)=\mathbb P(T_n>j)\qquad(0\le j<L_n).$$ At $j=L_n$, the probability on the right is zero, whereas $P_n(L_n)$ need not vanish. All statements below use only indices strictly below the cap when applying <a href="#cmp:prefix-endpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:prefix-endpoint">[cmp:prefix-endpoint]</a>.

<a id="stmt-cmp-convergence"></a>
<div id="cmp:convergence" class="theorem">

<a id="cmp:module-input"></a>
<a id="cmp:sum"></a>
<a id="cmp:neighbor"></a>
<a id="cmp:strict-sides"></a>
<a id="cmp:tree-bound"></a>
**Theorem 19** (Proper-clause center comparison from concentration). *<span id="thm:proper-center-module" label="thm:proper-center-module"></span><span id="cmp:modular" label="cmp:modular"></span> <span id="cmp:centers" label="cmp:centers"></span>Fix $k\ge3$, $B>0$, and $0<\delta<1/2$. Suppose that there are constants $0<a\le A<B$ and numbers $\eta_n\ge0$ tending to zero such that, for all sufficiently large $n$, $$\label{cmp:module-input}
 a\le e_n\le A,\qquad
 \mathbb P\bigl(|T_n-\mu_n|\ge n^{1-\delta}\bigr)\le\eta_n.$$ Then there is a finite constant $K\ge0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n=\min\{n_1,n_2\}$, with no restriction on $n_1/n_2$, $$\label{cmp:sum}
 e_{n_1+n_2}\ge\min\{e_{n_1},e_{n_2}\}-K n^{-\delta},$$ and, for all sufficiently large $n$, $$\label{cmp:neighbor}
 e_{n+1}\ge e_n-K n^{-\delta}.$$ Moreover $e_n$ converges to a number $\alpha\in[a,A]$, and $$\label{cmp:strict-sides}
 P_n(\lfloor cn\rfloor)\longrightarrow
 \begin{cases}1,&0\le c<\alpha,\\0,&c>\alpha.\end{cases}$$ For every sufficiently large $s$ and every $N\ge s^2$, one has the one-sided estimate $$\label{cmp:tree-bound}
 e_N\ge e_s-K(1+B_\delta)s^{-\delta},\qquad
 B_\delta=\frac{(3/2)^\delta}{1-(2/3)^\delta}.$$*

</div>

<a id="cmp:variance-input"></a>
The theorem allows an arbitrary vanishing concentration error; it does not require a specified polynomial rate. In particular, Chebyshev’s inequality supplies <a href="#cmp:module-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:module-input">[cmp:module-input]</a> whenever $$\label{cmp:variance-input}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(T_n)=o\bigl(n^{2-2\delta}\bigr).$$ The proof uses concentration twice: to make the two local formulas likely to be satisfiable, and to turn a likely satisfiable diluted formula back into a lower bound for the center at the combined size.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Local clauses and diluted global clauses

<a id="stmt-cmp-trial-comparison"></a>
<div id="cmp:trial-comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="cmp:weighted-replacement"></a>
<a id="cmp:coarse-replacement"></a>
<a id="cmp:neighbor-replacement"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Proper-clause trial comparison). *Partition $N=n_1+n_2$ variables into blocks of sizes $n_1,n_2\ge k$, and put $w_j=n_j/N$ and $n=\min\{n_1,n_2\}$. Fix an integer $M\ge0$ and $0<\varepsilon<1$. Each of $M$ independent local trials chooses block $j$ with probability $w_j$ and inserts an independent uniform proper $k$-clause in that block. Each of $M$ independent diluted global trials inserts an independent uniform proper $k$-clause on all $N$ variables with probability $1-\varepsilon$, and inserts no clause otherwise. Let $Q_{\mathrm{loc}}$ and $Q_{\mathrm{dil}}$ be the respective probabilities that the resulting formulas are satisfiable. Then $$\begin{aligned}
 Q_{\mathrm{dil}}
 &\ge Q_{\mathrm{loc}}-
 C_k M\varepsilon^{-k}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\frac{w_j}{n_j^k},
 & C_k&=\frac{[k(k-1)]^k}{2^k},\label{cmp:weighted-replacement}\\
 Q_{\mathrm{dil}}
 &\ge Q_{\mathrm{loc}}-\frac{C_kM}{\varepsilon^k n^k}.
 \label{cmp:coarse-replacement}
\end{aligned}$$ For $N=n+1$, if all local trials instead use a fixed main set of $n\ge k$ variables, then $$\label{cmp:neighbor-replacement}
 Q_{\mathrm{dil}}\ge Q_{\mathrm{loc}}
       -\frac{D_kM}{\varepsilon^k n^k},\qquad
 D_k=\frac{k^{2k}}{2^k}.$$ These statements impose no restriction on a block-size ratio.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cmp-trial-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-cmp-trial-comparison</summary>

<a id="cmp:factorial-bounds"></a>
*Proof.* Condition on every trial except the one being replaced. If the residual formula is unsatisfiable, both conditional satisfiability probabilities are zero. Otherwise let $S$ be its nonempty solution set, and call a variable frozen when it has the same value in every member of $S$. Among any specified $v\ge k$ variables of which $b$ are frozen, a proper $k$-clause kills $S$ with probability $$h_k(b,v)=\frac{(b)_k}{2^k(v)_k}.$$ Indeed, each of the selected variables must be frozen and each sign must oppose its frozen value. With $x=b/v$ and $y_+=\max\{y,0\}$, $$\label{cmp:factorial-bounds}
 \frac{(x-(k-1)/v)_+^k}{2^k}
 \le h_k(b,v)\le\frac{x^k}{2^k}.$$ For $b\ge k$, each factor $(b-r)/(v-r)$, $0\le r<k$, is at most $b/v$ and at least $(b-k+1)/v$. For $b<k$, both the middle expression and the positive part on the left vanish. This proves the bounds.

Let $\beta_j$ be the frozen fraction in block $j$, always with respect to the entire residual set $S$; its defining formula may contain clauses crossing the two blocks. By convexity and <a href="#cmp:factorial-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:factorial-bounds">[cmp:factorial-bounds]</a>, the killing probability of a nondiluted global clause is at most $$2^{-k}\left(\sum_jw_j\beta_j\right)^k
 \le 2^{-k}\sum_jw_j\beta_j^k,$$ whereas the local killing probability is at least $2^{-k}\sum_jw_j(\beta_j-(k-1)/n_j)_+^k$. If $\beta_j\ge k(k-1)/(\varepsilon n_j)$, Bernoulli’s inequality gives $$(\beta_j-(k-1)/n_j)_+^k
 \ge (1-\varepsilon/k)^k\beta_j^k
 \ge (1-\varepsilon)\beta_j^k.$$ Below that threshold, $\beta_j^k\le[k(k-1)]^k/(\varepsilon^k n_j^k)$. It follows that the diluted global killing probability exceeds the local one by at most $C_k\varepsilon^{-k}\sum_jw_j/n_j^k$. The estimate holds for every residual formula. Replacing the trials one at a time, conditioning on all other independent trials at each step, and then averaging and telescoping proves <a href="#cmp:weighted-replacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:weighted-replacement">[cmp:weighted-replacement]</a>. Since $n_j\ge n$ and $w_1+w_2=1$, it also proves the coarser bound.

For neighboring sizes, let $b$ count the frozen variables in the main $n$-set and let $x\in\{0,1\}$ indicate whether the extra variable is frozen. The global frozen fraction $\gamma=(b+x)/(n+1)$ obeys $b/n\ge\gamma-1/(n+1)$. The local killing probability is therefore at least $2^{-k}(\gamma-k/n)_+^k$, while the nondiluted global one is at most $2^{-k}\gamma^k$. Splitting at $\gamma=k^2/(\varepsilon n)$ and applying the same Bernoulli inequality bounds the per-trial loss by $D_k/(\varepsilon^k n^k)$. Telescoping gives <a href="#cmp:neighbor-replacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:neighbor-replacement">[cmp:neighbor-replacement]</a>. ◻

</details>

At $k=3$ the constants are exactly $C_3=27$ and $D_3=729/8$. The stronger weighted bound will be used for unequal blocks. The coarser bound remains useful when the block sizes are known to be comparable.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### From a random clause count to a center

<a id="cmp:concentration"></a>
We first record the integer step needed in both center comparisons. From <a href="#cmp:module-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:module-input">[cmp:module-input]</a> and <a href="#cmp:prefix-endpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:prefix-endpoint">[cmp:prefix-endpoint]</a>, for every integer $0\le j<L_n$ and all sufficiently large $n$, $$\label{cmp:concentration}
 \begin{aligned}
 j\le\mu_n-n^{1-\delta}&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad P_n(j)\ge1-\eta_n,\\
 j\ge\mu_n+n^{1-\delta}&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad P_n(j)\le\eta_n.
 \end{aligned}$$ Because $\eta_n\to0$, its tail supremum $\overline\eta_n=\sup_{v\ge n}\eta_v$ also tends to zero. This will provide uniformity when one block is arbitrarily larger than the other.

<a id="cmp:count-transfer"></a>
Suppose, for a fixed $a_0>0$, that $a_0N\le M\le AN$, $0<\varepsilon\le1/2$, and $Q_{\mathrm{dil}}\ge1-\zeta$. The number of inserted clauses is $J\sim\operatorname{Bin}(M,1-\varepsilon)$. Conditional on $J$, the inserted clauses have exactly the independent proper-clause law, so $Q_{\mathrm{dil}}=\mathbb EP_N(J)$. Put $$j=\left\lfloor(1-\varepsilon)M-N^{1-\delta}\right\rfloor.$$ For all sufficiently large $N$, the lower bound on $M$ gives $j\ge0$, and $j\le M\le AN<L_N$ because $A<B$. Also $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(J)\le AN$, whence $$\mathbb P(J<j)\le A N^{-1+2\delta},\qquad
 Q_{\mathrm{dil}}\le P_N(j)+\mathbb P(J<j).$$ The second inequality uses monotonicity of $P_N$. If $\zeta+AN^{-1+2\delta}+\eta_N<1$, the upper implication in <a href="#cmp:concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:concentration">[cmp:concentration]</a> forces $j<\mu_N+N^{1-\delta}$. The floor in the definition of $j$ then gives $$\label{cmp:count-transfer}
 e_N>\frac{(1-\varepsilon)M}{N}-2N^{-\delta}-N^{-1}
 \ge\frac{(1-\varepsilon)M}{N}-3N^{-\delta}.$$ We will apply this implication with all three errors tending to zero.

<details id="proof-stmt-cmp-trial-comparison-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-cmp-trial-comparison</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:proper-center-module" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:proper-center-module">[thm:proper-center-module]</a>.* For the two-block comparison, put $N=n_1+n_2$, $n=\min\{n_1,n_2\}$, $e_* =\min\{e_{n_1},e_{n_2}\}$, and choose $$\varepsilon=n^{-\delta},\qquad
 M=\left\lfloor(e_*-2\varepsilon)N\right\rfloor.$$ For sufficiently large $n$, the anchors imply $(a/4)N\le M\le AN$ and $\varepsilon\le1/2$. In the local model let $X_j$ count the clauses in block $j$. Its marginal law is binomial, and $$\mathbb EX_j=Mn_j/N\le\mu_{n_j}-2\varepsilon n_j
       \le\mu_{n_j}-2n_j^{1-\delta},\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(X_j)\le A n_j.$$ Thus $\mathbb P(X_j>\mu_{n_j}-n_j^{1-\delta})\le A n_j^{-1+2\delta}$. Conditional on the block choices, the clauses inside the two blocks have independent proper laws. On the event $X_j\le\mu_{n_j}-n_j^{1-\delta}$, the integer count lies below $L_{n_j}$ for large $n_j$, because $\mu_{n_j}\le A n_j$ and $A<B$. The lower implication in <a href="#cmp:concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:concentration">[cmp:concentration]</a> and a union bound therefore give $$Q_{\mathrm{loc}}\ge1-
 \sum_{j=1}^{2}\bigl(A n_j^{-1+2\delta}+\eta_{n_j}\bigr).$$ The right-hand error is at most $2A n^{-1+2\delta}+2\overline\eta_n$, which tends to zero uniformly over all pairs of sizes with minimum $n$.

The weighted replacement loss has the same uniformity: <a id="cmp:uniform-replacement-loss"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 C_kM\varepsilon^{-k}\sum_j\frac{w_j}{n_j^k}
 &=C_k\frac{M}{N}\varepsilon^{-k}\sum_jn_j^{1-k}\notag\\
 &\le2AC_k n^{1-k+k\delta}=o(1).
 \label{cmp:uniform-replacement-loss}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $1-k+k\delta<0$ for $k\ge3$ and $\delta<1/2$. The factor $N$ has canceled, so no upper bound on $N/n$ is used. Lemma <a href="#cmp:trial-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cmp:trial-comparison">20</a> therefore gives $Q_{\mathrm{dil}}\ge1-\zeta_n$, where $\zeta_n\to0$ uniformly over these pairs. Since $N\ge2n$, the count error at size $N$ and $\eta_N\le\overline\eta_n$ also vanish uniformly.
