Applying <a href="#cmp:count-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:count-transfer">[cmp:count-transfer]</a>, and keeping the floor in $M$, yields $$e_N\ge(1-\varepsilon)(e_*-2\varepsilon-N^{-1})-3N^{-\delta}
       \ge e_*-K n^{-\delta},$$ where the last inequality uses $e_*\le A$, $N\ge n$, and $\delta<1$. This proves <a href="#cmp:sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:sum">[cmp:sum]</a> for arbitrary size ratios.

For the neighboring comparison take $N=n+1$, put $\varepsilon=n^{-\delta}$, and let $M=\lfloor\mu_n-2n^{1-\delta}\rfloor$ local trials all use the main $n$ variables. Again $(a/4)N\le M\le AN$ for sufficiently large $n$. The local formula has exactly the law at size $n$ and count $M$, so $Q_{\mathrm{loc}}\ge1-\eta_n$ by <a href="#cmp:concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:concentration">[cmp:concentration]</a>. The neighboring replacement loss is $$\frac{D_kM}{\varepsilon^k n^k}=O_{k,A}(n^{1-k+k\delta})=o(1).$$ Equation <a href="#cmp:count-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:count-transfer">[cmp:count-transfer]</a> now gives $$e_{n+1}\ge(1-\varepsilon)
       \frac{ne_n-2n^{1-\delta}-1}{n+1}-3(n+1)^{-\delta}
       \ge e_n-Kn^{-\delta}.$$ For the last step, use $e_n\le A$ and $1-(1-\varepsilon)n/(n+1)\le\varepsilon+1/(n+1)$. This proves <a href="#cmp:neighbor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:neighbor">[cmp:neighbor]</a>.

It remains to explain the deterministic step and check its inputs. The geometric summation of errors parallels Abbe and Montanari’s approximate sum-superadditivity argument (Abbe and Montanari 2014, arXiv version, Lemma 8 and Remark 3) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[AM](#ref-AM)]. The required minimum comparison is the threshold companion’s Lemma 5.4 (“Balanced comparisons”) (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[FixedKThreshold](#ref-FixedKThreshold)]. For an integer $n_0\ge1$, it states that if a bounded real sequence $(u_n)_{n\ge n_0}$ satisfies $$u_{r+s}\ge\min\{u_r,u_s\}-K\min(r,s)^{-\delta}
 \quad\text{for }1/3\le r/s\le3,\qquad
 u_{s+1}\ge u_s-Ks^{-\delta},$$ for all $r,s\ge n_0$ in the indicated ratio range and all $s\ge n_0$, respectively, with fixed $K\ge0$ and $\delta>0$, then it converges and, for every $s\ge\max\{n_0,2\}$ and every $N\ge s^2$, $u_N\ge u_s-K(1+B_\delta)s^{-\delta}$ with $B_\delta$ as above. The lemma is a deterministic minimum comparison: it uses leaves of sizes $s,s+1$, and its binary tree has child-size ratios at most three. Choose $n_0$ beyond the cutoffs in our anchors and two comparisons. Then the boundedness in <a href="#cmp:module-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:module-input">[cmp:module-input]</a>, the comparable-size part of <a href="#cmp:sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:sum">[cmp:sum]</a>, and <a href="#cmp:neighbor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:neighbor">[cmp:neighbor]</a> verify every hypothesis with $u_n=e_n$. The lemma gives <a href="#cmp:tree-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:tree-bound">[cmp:tree-bound]</a> and convergence to some $\alpha\in[a,A]$.

For $0\le c<\alpha$, eventually $\lfloor cn\rfloor\le\mu_n-n^{1-\delta}$ and $\lfloor cn\rfloor<L_n$. For $\alpha<c<B$, eventually $\mu_n+n^{1-\delta}\le\lfloor cn\rfloor<L_n$. In both cases the fixed positive density gap eventually exceeds $n^{1-\delta}+1$, accounting for the floor. Apply <a href="#cmp:concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:concentration">[cmp:concentration]</a>. Since $\alpha\le A<B$, choose one fixed $d\in(A,B)$; monotonicity extends the zero limit from $d$ to all $c\ge B$. This proves <a href="#cmp:strict-sides" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:strict-sides">[cmp:strict-sides]</a>. ◻

</details>

The comparison <a href="#cmp:tree-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:tree-bound">[cmp:tree-bound]</a> is one-sided. It proves convergence of the finite-size means, but gives no two-sided estimate for $\mu_n-\alpha n$ on the fluctuation scale of the variance theorem.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Checking the inputs for the variance estimates

<a id="cmp:mean-upper"></a>
For the main application put $B_k=2^{k+1}$ and take $B=B_k$; at $k=3$ we may also take $B=10$. We verify the anchors without assuming threshold convergence. Let $q_k=1-2^{-k}$. The assignment first moment gives $P_n(j)\le2^nq_k^j$, hence, for every positive integer $d$, $$\label{cmp:mean-upper}
 \mathbb EH_n=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}P_n(j)
       \le dn+2^k(2q_k^d)^n.$$ For $d=2^k$, one has $2q_k^{2^k}<2/e<1$. For $k=3$, the sharper choice $d=6$ satisfies $2(7/8)^6<1$. Since $T_n\le H_n$, it follows that eventually <a id="cmp:center-upper"></a>$$\label{cmp:center-upper}
 e_n\le A_k<B,
 \qquad A_k=\begin{cases}7,&k=3,\\2^k+1,&k\ge4.\end{cases}$$

For the positive anchor, fix $0<c<\min\{e^{-2},B\}$ and let $j=\lfloor cn\rfloor$. Lemma <a href="#lem:sparse-satisfiability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sparse-satisfiability">15</a> gives $P_n(j)=1-O_c(n^{-2})$ for the same independent proper-clause model. Since $c<B$, one has $j<L_n$ for sufficiently large $n$. Therefore $\mu_n\ge jP_n(j)=cn-o(n)$, and with $c_0=c/2$ we obtain the full anchors <a id="cmp:center-bounds"></a>$$\label{cmp:center-bounds}
 c_0\le e_n\le A_k<B
 \qquad\text{for all sufficiently large }n.$$

The capped variance theorem gives $$\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(T_n)}{n^{2-2\delta}}
 =\begin{cases}
 O(n^{-1+2\delta}\log n),&k=3,\\
 O_k(n^{-1+2\delta}),&k\ge4,
 \end{cases}$$ which tends to zero for every fixed $0<\delta<1/2$. Theorem <a href="#thm:proper-center-module" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:proper-center-module">[thm:proper-center-module]</a> now applies with $a=c_0$ and $A=A_k$. Its strict-side limit is the same $\alpha_k$ as in the threshold companion (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[FixedKThreshold](#ref-FixedKThreshold)]: if the two limits differed, a density strictly between them would be assigned opposite limiting probabilities. In particular, the proper-clause comparisons <a href="#cmp:sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:sum">[cmp:sum]</a>–<a href="#cmp:neighbor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:neighbor">[cmp:neighbor]</a> hold for every such fixed $\delta$.

The weaker polynomial variance estimate of the threshold companion (OpenAI 2026a, Proposition 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[FixedKThreshold](#ref-FixedKThreshold)] can be used as a separate input. For $a=B_kn$ and the same last-SAT cap $b=a$, the exact identity in Proposition <a href="#prop:cap-bridge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cap-bridge">13</a> is $$\min\{H_n-1,a\}=\min\{H_n,a\}-1+\mathbf 1_{\{H_n>a\}}.$$ That proposition gives $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\min\{H_n,a\})\le\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\min\{H_n-1,a\})$. Consequently the companion’s $O_k(n^{1+2/k})$ bound verifies <a href="#cmp:variance-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:variance-input">[cmp:variance-input]</a> for every $0<\delta<(k-2)/(2k)$, including the companion’s stated choice $\delta=(k-2)/(4k)$. This substitution uses the companion’s own variance estimate. The range $\delta<1/2$ for the local variance bounds is also strict; no endpoint assertion is made.

<a id="sec:appendix-c"></a>
### A three-clause proof using prefix exposure

<span id="sec:prefix-exposure" label="sec:prefix-exposure"></span>

We give a second variance estimate for three-clause formulas, revealing clause types only through the current prefix. The argument truncates the number of clauses testing each variable of an omitted clause at $\lceil\log n\rceil$, and pays separately for clauses meeting two or more of those variables. It gives an $O(n\log^4 n)$ bound. The cube of the incidence cutoff contributes three logarithmic factors; the critical occupation integral contributes the fourth. The main argument in Sections <a href="#sec:deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:deletion">3</a>–<a href="#sec:occupation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:occupation">4</a> instead averages the bounded third moment of the actual incidence count, giving $O(n\log n)$. Comparing the two proofs shows where that improvement enters. The replacement step below also gives a distinct finite-variable estimate: replacing one two-clause on $u$ variables by $g$ three-clauses loses at most $64(g^{-2}+u^{-2})$ in killing probability.

Throughout this section, $C_1,C_2,\ldots$ are independent uniform proper three-clauses on $n\ge3$ variables: a clause uses three distinct variables and independent fair signs, while whole clauses may repeat. Write $H_n$ for the index of the first unsatisfiable prefix and put $$L=10n,\qquad T_n=\min\{H_n,L\},\qquad m_n=\mathbb ET_n.$$

<a id="stmt-thm-prefix-variance"></a>
<div id="thm:prefix-variance" class="theorem">

**Theorem 21** (Variance from prefix exposure). *There is an absolute constant $C$ such that, for all integers $n\ge3$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(T_n)\le Cn(\log n)^4.$$ Consequently, for every $t>0$, $$\mathbb P\bigl(|T_n-m_n|\ge t\sqrt n(\log n)^2\bigr)\le Ct^{-2}.$$*

</div>

The proof has two uses of the same lifetime estimate. It bounds the remaining duration of a deletion event and the total time spent at a given level of the probability of killing the surviving assignments. Three independent root-flip tests connect those two bounds.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Replacing pairs by triples on a finite variable set

For an integer $u\ge3$ and $S\subseteq\{0,1\}^u$, a collection of clauses *kills* $S$ if no member of $S$ satisfies all of them. A uniform proper $r$-clause here is chosen from the $2^r\binom ur$ signed clauses on $r$ distinct variables. For an integer $d\ge1$, let $q_d(S)$ be the probability that $d$ independent uniform proper two-clauses kill $S$. Let $\tau(S)$ be the expected length of the first prefix of independent uniform proper three-clauses whose conjunction kills $S$, and set $\tau(\varnothing)=0$. For nonempty $S$, this expectation is finite because the probability of survival after $m$ clauses is at most $|S|(7/8)^m$.

Replacing a shorter constraint by native clauses has a qualitative predecessor in Friedgut’s half-cube argument (Friedgut 1999, Lemma 5.7) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Friedgut](#ref-Friedgut)]. The residual-set conditioning used below is the averaged replacement method of Carenini (Carenini 2026b, Theorem 4.3, Lemma 6.1, and Corollary 6.2) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Carenini](#ref-Carenini)]; the following bound supplies the finite-variable pair-to-triple estimate needed here.

<a id="stmt-lem-prefix-one-pair"></a>
<div id="lem:prefix-one-pair" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:prefix-one-pair"></a>
**Lemma 22** (One-pair replacement). *For every integer $u\ge3$, every $S\subseteq\{0,1\}^u$, and every integer $g\ge1$, if the $g$ triples are independent uniform proper three-clauses on these $u$ variables, then $$\label{eq:prefix-one-pair}
 \mathbb P(\text{the \(g\) triples kill \(S\)})
 \ge q_1(S)-64(g^{-2}+u^{-2}).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prefix-one-pair"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prefix-one-pair</summary>

*Proof.* If $S=\varnothing$, both killing probabilities are one. Suppose that $S\ne\varnothing$, and let $b$ be the number of variables taking a common value on all members of $S$. A single clause kills $S$ exactly when every one of its variables is among these $b$ frozen variables and every sign opposes the common value. Thus the killing probability of a proper $r$-clause is $$h_r=\frac{(b)_r}{2^r(u)_r},\qquad r=2,3,$$ where $(x)_r=x(x-1)\cdots(x-r+1)$, and $(b)_r=0$ for integers $0\le b<r$. For $b\ge3$, $$\frac{h_3}{h_2^{3/2}}
 =\frac{b-2}{\sqrt{b(b-1)}}\frac{\sqrt{u(u-1)}}{u-2}
 \ge\frac14.$$ Indeed, the first factor is at least $(b-2)/b\ge1/3$, and the second is at least one.

Put $h=h_2\le1/4$. The block kills $S$ whenever one of its clauses kills $S$ by itself, so its killing probability is at least $1-(1-h_3)^g\ge1-e^{-gh_3}$. If $b\ge3$ and $(g/4)\sqrt h\ge2$, then $gh_3\ge2h$, and this probability is at least $1-e^{-2h}\ge h$. The last inequality follows because $1-e^{-2h}-h$ vanishes at zero and has nonnegative derivative on $[0,1/4]$. In the remaining case with $b\ge3$, $h\le64g^{-2}$. If $b<3$, then $$h\le\frac1{2u(u-1)}\le u^{-2}.$$ The nonnegativity of the block killing probability now proves <a href="#eq:prefix-one-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-one-pair">[eq:prefix-one-pair]</a> in every case. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

**Remark 23**. For $k=3$, the cutoff-free estimate in Lemma <a href="#lem:one-replacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:one-replacement">2</a> has error $256g^{-2}$. Neither that error nor $64(g^{-2}+u^{-2})$ is uniformly smaller: the latter is smaller for $g<\sqrt3\,u$, the former is smaller for $g>\sqrt3\,u$, and they are equal when $g=\sqrt3\,u$. The two estimates therefore retain different quantitative information.

</div>

<a id="stmt-prop-prefix-lifetime"></a>
<div id="prop:prefix-lifetime" class="proposition">

**Proposition 24** (Lifetime above the finite-variable cutoff). *Let $K=64$. There is an absolute constant $K_1$ such that for every integer $u\ge3$, every nonempty $S\subseteq\{0,1\}^u$, every integer $d\ge1$, and every $a$ with $4Kd/u^2\le a\le1$, <a id="eq:prefix-lifetime"></a>$$\label{eq:prefix-lifetime}
 q_d(S)\ge a\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \tau(S)\le K_1d^{3/2}a^{-3/2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-prefix-lifetime"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-prefix-lifetime</summary>

*Proof.* Replace $d$ independent two-clauses one at a time by independent blocks of $g$ proper three-clauses. At each step condition on the clauses in every other position, including the blocks already inserted. Their surviving assignments form a set $S'\subseteq S$. The pair being removed and the new block are independent of this conditioning. Lemma <a href="#lem:prefix-one-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prefix-one-pair">22</a>, including its empty-set case, bounds the conditional loss by $K(g^{-2}+u^{-2})$. Averaging and telescoping over the replacements gives <a id="eq:prefix-many-pairs"></a>$$\label{eq:prefix-many-pairs}
 \mathbb P(\text{\(dg\) independent uniform proper three-clauses kill \(S\)})
 \ge q_d(S)-Kd(g^{-2}+u^{-2}).$$ Choose $g=\lceil\sqrt{4Kd/a}\rceil$. Each of $Kd/g^2$ and $Kd/u^2$ is at most $a/4$, so a block of $dg$ triples kills the original set $S$ with probability at least $a/2$. In an infinite independent sequence, disjoint blocks have independent events of killing that original set. Their first successful block has expected index at most $2/a$, and the cumulative sequence kills $S$ by the end of that block. Therefore $$\tau(S)\le\frac{2dg}{a}\le 8\sqrt K\,d^{3/2}a^{-3/2},$$ where $g\le2\sqrt{4Kd/a}$ follows from $d\ge1$ and $a\le1$. This proves the assertion, for example with $K_1=64$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### A deleted clause and an unexposed future

<a id="eq:prefix-omission"></a>
We now apply the lifetime estimate to the delay caused by omitting one clause. We first assume $n$ is sufficiently large, in particular $n\ge6$; the final constant will cover the remaining sizes. Keep the original clause indices, and for $1\le i\le L$ and integer $m\ge0$ put $$B_m^{(i)}=\bigwedge_{\substack{1\le j\le m\\j\ne i}}C_j,
 \qquad
 T^{(i)}=\min\bigl\{\inf\{m\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}:
                   B_m^{(i)}\notin\mathrm{SAT}\},L\bigr\},
 \qquad D_i=T^{(i)}-T_n,$$ with $\inf\varnothing=\infty$. Then $D_i\ge0$, and $T^{(i)}$ does not use coordinate $C_i$. The coordinate-omission inequality, Lemma <a href="#lem:coordinate-omission" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coordinate-omission">5</a>, gives $$\label{eq:prefix-omission}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(T_n)\le\sum_{i=1}^{L}\mathbb ED_i^2.$$ Its martingale proof applies to precisely these index-preserving deletions and to the cap $L=10n$.

Fix $i$ and condition on any particular clause $C_i$. Until the end of the variance proof, probabilities refer to this conditional law; the other clauses remain independent and uniform. We call indices other than $i$ the baseline indices for this deletion. Let $R$ be the three variables of $C_i$, called its *roots*, and let $t\in\{0,1\}^{R}$ be their unique values that falsify $C_i$. Put $u=n-3$, abbreviate $B_m=B_m^{(i)}$, and let $\mathcal F_m=\sigma(C_j:1\le j\le m,\ j\ne i)$ under the conditional law. In particular, $u\ge3$.

<a id="eq:prefix-delay-events"></a>
<a id="eq:prefix-delay-sum"></a>
For $i\le m<L$, define $$\label{eq:prefix-delay-events}
 A_m=\{T_n\le m<T^{(i)}\}
     =\{B_m\in\mathrm{SAT},\ B_m\wedge C_i\notin\mathrm{SAT}\}.$$ On $A_m$, every solution of $B_m$ has root values $t$. The two processes agree before index $i$, so $$\label{eq:prefix-delay-sum}
 D_i=\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}\mathbf 1_{\{A_m\}}.$$ In particular $D_L=0$. For every $m\ge i$, let $S_m\subseteq\{0,1\}^u$ be the nonroot restrictions of solutions of $B_m$ with root values $t$. The sets are decreasing, $\mathcal F_m$-measurable, and nonempty on $A_m$.

<a id="eq:prefix-future-mean"></a>
A clause is *ordinary* if it uses only nonroots. A fresh clause is ordinary with probability $$p_0=\frac{\binom{u}{3}}{\binom n3},$$ which is bounded below by a positive absolute constant for sufficiently large $n$. Let $U_m$ be the number of trials after index $m$, including the last trial, until the ordinary clauses among them kill the original set $S_m$; put $U_m=0$ when $S_m=\varnothing$. These trials may continue past $L$. Conditional on $\mathcal F_m$, the future ordinary clauses are independent uniform proper triples on the nonroots. Their arrival gaps, counting the arrival trial, are independent geometric variables of mean $1/p_0$, independent of the ordinary clauses themselves. Conditioning first on the latter clauses and summing the means of the gaps through their killing time proves $$\label{eq:prefix-future-mean}
 \mathbb E[U_m\mid\mathcal F_m]=\frac{\tau(S_m)}{p_0}.$$ The assertion uses the fact that only the prefix has been revealed; for $m\ge i$, all trials after $m$ remain fresh even after fixing $C_i$.

<a id="stmt-lem-prefix-future-delay"></a>
<div id="lem:prefix-future-delay" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:prefix-weighted-delay"></a>
**Lemma 25** (Remaining deletion time). *Set $\mathcal W_m=\min\{L,\tau(S_m)/p_0\}$. For $i\le m<L$, on $A_m$, $$\mathbb E\left[\sum_{\ell=m+1}^{L-1}\mathbf 1_{\{A_\ell\}}
                  \,\middle|\,\mathcal F_m\right]\le\mathcal W_m.$$ Consequently, $$\label{eq:prefix-weighted-delay}
 \mathbb ED_i^2\le3\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_{\{A_m\}}\mathcal W_m].$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prefix-future-delay"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prefix-future-delay</summary>

*Proof.* On $A_m$, any later solution of $B_\ell$ still has root values $t$, and its nonroot part lies in $S_m$. Once the subsequent ordinary clauses kill $S_m$, no such solution remains. Thus $A_\ell$ is impossible for $\ell\ge m+U_m$. The number of later indices is at most both $U_m$ and $L$; its conditional expectation is therefore at most $\min\{\mathbb E[U_m\mid\mathcal F_m],L\}$. Equation <a href="#eq:prefix-future-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-future-mean">[eq:prefix-future-mean]</a> proves the first assertion.

On $A_m$, the nonempty set $S_m$ requires at least one triple to be killed, so $\mathcal W_m\ge1$. Expanding <a href="#eq:prefix-delay-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-delay-sum">[eq:prefix-delay-sum]</a>, conditioning each cross term on its earlier history, and using the first assertion gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb ED_i^2
 &=\sum_m\mathbb E\mathbf 1_{\{A_m\}}
   +2\sum_m\mathbb E\left[\mathbf 1_{\{A_m\}}
      \mathbb E\left[\sum_{\ell=m+1}^{L-1}\mathbf 1_{\{A_\ell\}}
                  \,\middle|\,\mathcal F_m\right]\right]\\
 &\le\sum_m\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_{\{A_m\}}(1+2\mathcal W_m)]
 \le3\sum_m\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_{\{A_m\}}\mathcal W_m],
\end{aligned}$$ where every outer sum runs from $m=i$ to $L-1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### The three root-flip tests

The weight in <a href="#eq:prefix-weighted-delay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-weighted-delay">[eq:prefix-weighted-delay]</a> is determined by the surviving set. To estimate the accompanying deletion probability, we hide the parts of clauses that are already satisfied at the fixed root values. They will test whether a root can be flipped.

The *type* of a baseline clause through index $m$ records the roots it contains and the signs of its root literals. For $j\in R$, let $\mathcal I_{j,m}$ be the indices other than $i$, at most $m$, whose clause contains exactly root $j$ and whose root literal is true at $t$; put $s_{j,m}=|\mathcal I_{j,m}|$. Call a clause containing at least two roots a *collision*, and let $v_m$ count such clauses at baseline indices through $m$. Finally, let $J_m\subseteq R$ contain those roots $j$ for which no collision through $m$ has exactly one root literal true under $t$, at root $j$. A collision can exclude at most one root, hence <a id="eq:prefix-available-roots"></a>$$\label{eq:prefix-available-roots}
 |J_m|\ge3-v_m.$$

Let $\mathcal G_m$ reveal all these prefix types and the entire signed nonroot part of every baseline clause through $m$, except at indices in $\bigcup_{j\in R}\mathcal I_{j,m}$. Thus $\mathcal G_m\subseteq\mathcal F_m$. The hidden clauses are already satisfied by their root literal at $t$, so their nonroot parts do not affect $S_m$. In particular $S_m,J_m,s_{j,m},v_m$, and $\mathcal W_m$ are $\mathcal G_m$-measurable. Conditional on $\mathcal G_m$, the hidden nonroot parts are independent uniform proper two-clauses on the $u$ nonroots. Indeed, conditioning the independent clauses on their types separately preserves their product law, and revealing nonroot parts at other indices does not change it.

Put $$d=\lceil\log n\rceil,\qquad q_m=q_d(S_m).$$

<a id="stmt-lem-prefix-root-tests"></a>
<div id="lem:prefix-root-tests" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:prefix-root-tests"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Root-flip tests under prefix conditioning). *For every $i\le m<L$, on the $\mathcal G_m$-measurable event $\{\max_{j\in R}s_{j,m}\le d,\ |J_m|\ge1\}$, $$\label{eq:prefix-root-tests}
 \mathbb P(A_m\mid\mathcal G_m)
 \le\mathbf 1_{\{S_m\ne\varnothing\}}q_m^{|J_m|}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prefix-root-tests"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prefix-root-tests</summary>

*Proof.* If $A_m$ occurs, then for each $j\in J_m$ the pair clauses at indices in $\mathcal I_{j,m}$ must kill $S_m$. Otherwise choose an assignment in $S_m$ satisfying those pairs, extend it with root values $t$, and flip only $j$. A clause with no true root literal before the flip has a satisfied nonroot part, which is unchanged. A clause with a true root literal at a root other than $j$ remains satisfied. Any remaining clause has $j$ as its unique true root before the flip. It cannot be a collision because $j\in J_m$, so it belongs to $\mathcal I_{j,m}$ and its pair is satisfied by the chosen assignment. The flipped assignment therefore satisfies $B_m$ with root values different from $t$, contradicting <a href="#eq:prefix-delay-events" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-delay-events">[eq:prefix-delay-events]</a>.

The required killing events for different $j$ use disjoint families of the conditionally independent hidden pairs. On a nonempty $S_m$, each family has at most $d$ members and therefore kills with probability at most $q_d(S_m)=q_m$. A family of zero pairs has killing probability zero, so it causes no exception. Multiplication of these conditional bounds, together with the necessity of $S_m\ne\varnothing$, proves <a href="#eq:prefix-root-tests" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-root-tests">[eq:prefix-root-tests]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Truncating incidence counts and summing occupation

It remains to sum the right side of <a href="#eq:prefix-weighted-delay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-weighted-delay">[eq:prefix-weighted-delay]</a>. The tests make deletion unlikely when $q_m$ is small. When it is large, the lifetime estimate limits both the remaining deletion time and the number of indices at that level. We first isolate the rare prefixes for which too many tests or collisions are present.

<a id="stmt-lem-prefix-counts"></a>
<div id="lem:prefix-counts" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27** (Prefix incidence counts). *Uniformly in $i\le m<L$ and in the fixed value of $C_i$, <a id="eq:prefix-large-count"></a><a id="eq:prefix-collision-counts"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P\bigl(\max_{j\in R}s_{j,m}>d\bigr)&=O(n^{-4}),
 \label{eq:prefix-large-count}\\
 \mathbb P(v_m\ge1)&=O(n^{-1}),\qquad
 \mathbb P(v_m\ge2)=O(n^{-2}).
 \label{eq:prefix-collision-counts}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prefix-counts"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prefix-counts</summary>

*Proof.* A baseline clause contains a specified root with probability $3/n$, independently across indices, and $s_{j,m}$ is no greater than that root’s incidence count. A union bound over roots and choices of $d$ indices among at most $L=10n$ trials gives <a id="eq:prefix-count-numerical"></a>$$\label{eq:prefix-count-numerical}
 \mathbb P\bigl(\max_{j\in R}s_{j,m}>d\bigr)
 \le3\binom Ld(3/n)^d\le3(30e/d)^d=O(n^{-4}).$$ The final bound holds for sufficiently large $n$, since $d=\lceil\log n\rceil$ and $\log d\to\infty$.

A trial contains any specified pair of roots with probability $6/[n(n-1)]$. The union over the three pairs bounds its collision probability by $18/[n(n-1)]$. A union bound over at most $L$ independent trials gives $\mathbb P(v_m\ge1)=O(n^{-1})$; a union bound over pairs of trials gives $\mathbb P(v_m\ge2)\le\binom L2 O(n^{-4})=O(n^{-2})$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:prefix-weighted-lifetime"></a>
<a id="eq:prefix-small-level"></a>
With $K=64$, set <a id="eq:prefix-cutoff"></a>$$\label{eq:prefix-cutoff}
 a_0=\frac{4Kd}{u^2}=\frac{256d}{(n-3)^2}.$$ For sufficiently large $n$, $a_0<1$. On $S_m\ne\varnothing$, $$\label{eq:prefix-weighted-lifetime}
 \mathcal W_m q_m^{3/2}\le C d^{3/2}.$$ For $q_m\ge a_0$, this follows by applying Proposition <a href="#prop:prefix-lifetime" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prefix-lifetime">24</a> with $a=q_m$, and then using $\mathcal W_m\le\tau(S_m)/p_0$. For $q_m<a_0$, including $q_m=0$, use $\mathcal W_m\le L$ and $$\label{eq:prefix-small-level}
 L a_0^{3/2}
 =10n\left(\frac{256d}{(n-3)^2}\right)^{3/2}
 =O(d^{3/2}n^{-2})\le C d^{3/2}.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-prefix-reduction"></a>
<div id="lem:prefix-reduction" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:prefix-reduction"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Reduction to occupation). *For all sufficiently large $n$, uniformly in $i$ and in the value of $C_i$, $$\label{eq:prefix-reduction}
 \mathbb ED_i^2\le C(1+d^{3/2})+
 C d^{3/2}\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}
       \mathbb E\bigl[\mathbf 1_{\{S_m\ne\varnothing\}}q_m^{3/2}\bigr].$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prefix-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prefix-reduction</summary>

*Proof.* In <a href="#eq:prefix-weighted-delay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-weighted-delay">[eq:prefix-weighted-delay]</a>, the terms on $\{\max_j s_{j,m}>d\}\cup\{v_m\ge2\}$ contribute at most $$L\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}
 \bigl[\mathbb P(\max_j s_{j,m}>d)+\mathbb P(v_m\ge2)\bigr]
 =O\bigl(L^2(n^{-4}+n^{-2})\bigr)=O(1).$$ For every remaining term, the restrictions, $\mathcal W_m$, and $S_m$ are $\mathcal G_m$-measurable. If $v_m=1$, then $|J_m|\ge2$. Conditioning on $\mathcal G_m$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:prefix-root-tests" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prefix-root-tests">26</a> bounds its weighted probability by $\mathbf 1_{\{S_m\ne\varnothing\}}\mathcal W_mq_m^2$. On a nonempty set, <a href="#eq:prefix-weighted-lifetime" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-weighted-lifetime">[eq:prefix-weighted-lifetime]</a> and $q_m\le1$ bound this by $Cd^{3/2}$. The one-collision contribution is therefore at most $$Cd^{3/2}\sum_{m=i}^{L-1}\mathbb P(v_m=1)=O(d^{3/2}).$$ No independence between $v_m$ and the surviving set is asserted or needed.

If $v_m=0$, all three roots are available, and the same conditional bound has exponent three. By <a href="#eq:prefix-weighted-lifetime" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-weighted-lifetime">[eq:prefix-weighted-lifetime]</a>, $$\mathbf 1_{\{S_m\ne\varnothing\}}\mathcal W_mq_m^3
 \le Cd^{3/2}\mathbf 1_{\{S_m\ne\varnothing\}}q_m^{3/2}.$$ We may now discard the incidence restrictions. Summing the three cases and absorbing the factor three from <a href="#eq:prefix-weighted-delay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-weighted-delay">[eq:prefix-weighted-delay]</a> proves <a href="#eq:prefix-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-reduction">[eq:prefix-reduction]</a>. ◻

</details>

We next bound how long the decreasing sets $S_m$ can remain nonempty at a given pair-killing level. The first visit to that level determines a set whose future lifetime controls all subsequent visits.

<a id="stmt-lem-prefix-occupation"></a>
<div id="lem:prefix-occupation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:prefix-occupation"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Occupation above a level). *For all sufficiently large $n$ and every $a_0\le a\le1$, $$\label{eq:prefix-occupation}
 \sum_{m=i}^{L-1}\mathbb P(S_m\ne\varnothing,\ q_m\ge a)
 \le Cd^{3/2}a^{-3/2}.$$ For $0<a<a_0$, the same sum is at most $L$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prefix-occupation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prefix-occupation</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\sigma_a$ be the first index in $\{i,\ldots,L-1\}$ for which $S_m\ne\varnothing$ and $q_m\ge a$, and set $\sigma_a=\infty$ when none exists. This is a stopping time for $\mathcal F_m$, because $q_d$ is a deterministic function of its set argument. On $\{\sigma_a=m\}$, every counted index is at least $m$. Also $S_\ell=\varnothing$ for $\ell\ge m+U_m$: by then the future ordinary clauses have killed $S_m$, and any member of $S_\ell$ would be a member of $S_m$ satisfying all those clauses. The number of counted indices is thus at most $U_m$, including the first index $m$.

For $a\ge a_0$, the event $\{\sigma_a=m\}$ is $\mathcal F_m$-measurable, and on it the lifetime proposition applies. The fresh-future identity gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[\mathbf 1_{\{\sigma_a=m\}}U_m]
 &=\mathbb E\left[\mathbf 1_{\{\sigma_a=m\}}\frac{\tau(S_m)}{p_0}\right]\\
 &\le Cd^{3/2}a^{-3/2}\mathbb P(\sigma_a=m).
\end{aligned}$$ Summing over the disjoint first-visit events proves <a href="#eq:prefix-occupation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-occupation">[eq:prefix-occupation]</a>. This conditioning is legitimate because $m\ge i$ and all trials after $m$ remain unexposed. At a smaller level there are at most $L$ indices to count. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prefix-occupation-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prefix-occupation</summary>

<a id="eq:prefix-squared-influence"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:prefix-variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:prefix-variance">21</a>.* For $0\le q\le1$, $q^{3/2}=\int_0^1\frac32a^{1/2}\mathbf 1_{\{q\ge a\}}\,da$. Applying this identity and Lemma <a href="#lem:prefix-occupation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prefix-occupation">29</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{m=i}^{L-1}\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_{\{S_m\ne\varnothing\}}q_m^{3/2}]
 &=\int_0^1\frac32a^{1/2}
       \sum_{m=i}^{L-1}\mathbb P(S_m\ne\varnothing,q_m\ge a)\,da\\
 &\le L a_0^{3/2}+Cd^{3/2}\int_{a_0}^1\frac{da}{a}
 \le Cd^{3/2}\log n.
\end{aligned}$$ The final step uses <a href="#eq:prefix-small-level" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-small-level">[eq:prefix-small-level]</a> and $\log(1/a_0)\le2\log n$, since $a_0\ge n^{-2}$. The sum is finite and the integrands are nonnegative, so the interchange of sum, expectation, and integral is valid. Lemma <a href="#lem:prefix-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prefix-reduction">28</a> now yields, uniformly in the deleted index and the fixed clause, $$\label{eq:prefix-squared-influence}
 \mathbb ED_i^2\le C(1+d^{3/2})+Cd^3\log n\le C(\log n)^4.$$ Average over $C_i$ to remove the conditioning, and sum <a href="#eq:prefix-squared-influence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-squared-influence">[eq:prefix-squared-influence]</a> in <a href="#eq:prefix-omission" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-omission">[eq:prefix-omission]</a>. This gives $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(T_n)\le Cn(\log n)^4$ for sufficiently large $n$. Enlarging $C$ covers the remaining integers $n\ge3$, since $0\le T_n\le10n$. Chebyshev’s inequality proves the stated tail bound. ◻

</details>

For every fixed $0<\eta<1/2$, the $\eta$ and $1-\eta$ quantiles of $T_n$ therefore lie within $O_\eta(\sqrt n\log^2 n)$ of $m_n$. The probability outside any diverging multiple of this scale tends to zero. These statements use the finite-size mean $m_n$.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Cap changes and the center comparison

The prefix proof supplies a second concentration input for Theorem <a href="#thm:proper-center-module" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:proper-center-module">[thm:proper-center-module]</a>. That theorem assumes a concentration bound and proves the center comparisons independently of how the bound is obtained. We apply it here using the variance bound of Theorem <a href="#thm:prefix-variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:prefix-variance">21</a>, together with the cap identities already proved in Section <a href="#sec:three-clause-refinements" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:three-clause-refinements">7</a>. Put $$W_n=\min\{H_n-1,10n\},\qquad V_n=\min\{H_n-1,16n\},
 \qquad e_n=\frac{\mathbb ET_n}{n},\qquad P_n(j)=\mathbb P(H_n>j).$$

<a id="stmt-cor-prefix-consequences"></a>
<div id="cor:prefix-consequences" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:prefix-center-comparisons"></a>
**Corollary 30**. *The variance and tail bounds of Theorem <a href="#thm:prefix-variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:prefix-variance">21</a> hold also for $W_n$ and $V_n$, each centered at its own expectation. Moreover $e_n\to\alpha_3$, the strict-side threshold of the proper three-clause model: for every fixed $c\ge0$ with $c\ne\alpha_3$, $$P_n(\lfloor cn\rfloor)\longrightarrow
 \begin{cases}1,&c<\alpha_3,\\0,&c>\alpha_3.\end{cases}$$ There is an absolute constant $C$ such that for all sufficiently large $n=\min\{n_1,n_2\}$ with $\max\{n_1,n_2\}\le3n$, and for all sufficiently large $n$ in the second inequality, $$\label{eq:prefix-center-comparisons}
 e_{n_1+n_2}\ge\min\{e_{n_1},e_{n_2}\}-Cn^{-1/4},
 \qquad e_{n+1}\ge e_n-Cn^{-1/4}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-prefix-consequences"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-prefix-consequences</summary>

*Proof.* Set $\rho=2(7/8)^{10}<1$. With $T=T_n$, $W=W_n$, and $V=V_n$, identities <a href="#eq:three-same-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:three-same-cap">[eq:three-same-cap]</a>–<a href="#eq:three-cross-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:three-cross-cap">[eq:three-cross-cap]</a> show that the mean corrections from $m_n-1$ are at most $\rho^n$ and $(6n+1)\rho^n$, respectively. The corresponding absolute differences of standard deviations are at most $\rho^{n/2}$ and $(6n+1)\rho^{n/2}$; the sharper geometric bounds are those in Section <a href="#sec:three-clause-refinements" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:three-clause-refinements">7</a>, obtained from Proposition <a href="#prop:cap-bridge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cap-bridge">13</a>. These exponentially small corrections transfer the variance estimate to both last-satisfiable statistics. Chebyshev’s inequality gives their tails about their own means.

For the center claim, choose a fixed $0<c<e^{-2}$ and put $j=\lfloor cn\rfloor$. Lemma <a href="#lem:sparse-satisfiability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sparse-satisfiability">15</a> gives $\mathbb ET_n\ge jP_n(j)=cn-o(n)$. The survival-sum bound <a href="#cmp:mean-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:mean-upper">[cmp:mean-upper]</a> with $k=3$ and density six gives $\mathbb ET_n\le\mathbb EH_n\le6n+8[2(7/8)^6]^n$. Thus $c_0\le e_n\le7<10$ eventually, for some absolute $c_0>0$. The relevant survival identity is $P_n(j)=\mathbb P(T_n>j)$ for integers $0\le j<10n$; the restriction below the cap is essential, whereas the last-SAT identity $P_n(j)=\mathbb P(W_n\ge j)$ includes $j=10n$. Theorem <a href="#thm:prefix-variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:prefix-variance">21</a> supplies the concentration input <a id="eq:prefix-center-concentration"></a>$$\label{eq:prefix-center-concentration}
 \mathbb P\bigl(|T_n-m_n|\ge n^{3/4}\bigr)\le\eta_n,
 \qquad \eta_n=C\frac{(\log n)^4}{\sqrt n}\longrightarrow0.$$ Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:proper-center-module" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:proper-center-module">[thm:proper-center-module]</a> with $k=3$, $B=10$, $a=c_0$, $A=7$, and $\delta=1/4$. It gives both comparisons in <a href="#eq:prefix-center-comparisons" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-center-comparisons">[eq:prefix-center-comparisons]</a>, in particular for the stated comparable sizes, and convergence with the proper strict-side limits. Uniqueness identifies the limit with $\alpha_3$ of (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[FixedKThreshold](#ref-FixedKThreshold)]. The one-sided estimate <a href="#cmp:tree-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:tree-bound">[cmp:tree-bound]</a> also gives, for every sufficiently large $s$ and every $N\ge s^2$, $$e_N\ge e_s-C's^{-1/4}.$$ ◻

</details>

For the comparable-size consequence, the coarser trial comparison gives a concrete error bound. With $N=n_1+n_2$, $n=\min(n_1,n_2)$, $e_*=\min(e_{n_1},e_{n_2})$, set $\varepsilon=n^{-1/4}$ and $M=\lfloor(e_*-2\varepsilon)N\rfloor$, as in the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:proper-center-module" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:proper-center-module">[thm:proper-center-module]</a>. The anchors give $(c_0/4)N\le M\le7N$ for large $n$. Since $C_3=27$ in Lemma <a href="#cmp:trial-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cmp:trial-comparison">20</a> and $N\le4n$, <a href="#cmp:coarse-replacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:coarse-replacement">[cmp:coarse-replacement]</a> bounds the loss by <a id="eq:prefix-756-loss"></a>$$\label{eq:prefix-756-loss}
 \frac{27M}{\varepsilon^3n^3}
 \le\frac{27\cdot7\cdot4n}{n^{9/4}}
 =756n^{-5/4}.$$ The restriction $\max(n_1,n_2)\le3n$ is used only for this numerical bound. For neighboring sizes, set $N=n+1$ and $M=\lfloor m_n-2n^{3/4}\rfloor$. The constant $D_3=729/8$ gives loss $(729/8)M/(\varepsilon^3n^3)=O(n^{-5/4})$. In either case, the count-transfer index $j=\lfloor(1-\varepsilon)M-N^{3/4}\rfloor$ is nonnegative for large $n$ and lies strictly below $10N$, as required in <a href="#cmp:count-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:count-transfer">[cmp:count-transfer]</a>.

The deviation bounds above are about each statistic’s finite-size mean. The comparison inequalities are one-sided and do not give a two-sided rate for $e_n-\alpha_3$.
